WALK THIS WAY - Chapter 1 (Revised)
Chapter 1: The Last Voice of Boston Rock
The thing about endings is they make liars of us all. We promise to go out with dignity, to say something profound, to make those final moments count. But here I am, Maxanne Sartori, seventy-three years old, sitting in the same Studio C where I discovered Aerosmith in 1973, and all I can think about is two kids who never made it onto my playlist.
WBCN dies at midnight. After fifty-six years of being the Rock of Boston, we're flipping to automated Christian contemporary. The equipment around me—turntables I fought to keep when everything went digital, cart machines that hold the DNA of Boston rock, mixing boards that have channeled more raw talent than any studio in America—will be sold for scrap or shipped to museums where teenagers will stare at them like archaeological artifacts.
"Maxanne?" The program director's voice crackles through the intercom. He's twenty-eight, wears sneakers that cost more than my first car, and thinks rock history started with Nirvana. "Four hours left. Make them count."
Make them count. As if four hours could contain what WBCN meant. As if I could summarize the moment when rock and roll was dangerous, when DJ voices mattered more than algorithms, when two kids with guitars could change the world if they could just stop resenting each other long enough to plug in their amps.
I adjust the headphones—the same model I've worn since 1971, held together now with electrical tape and pure stubbornness. The ON AIR sign flashes red. One last time.
"This is Maxanne Sartori, and for what remains of this October night, you're listening to the final broadcast of WBCN, 104.1 on your FM dial. I've been your guide through Boston rock since before some of you were born. I gave Springsteen his first radio interview in '73 when he was just another Jersey kid with a guitar and something to prove. He played 'Rosalita' live in this booth, just him and Clarence on sax, and I knew—the same way I knew with Tyler—that something real was happening."
I take a sip of bourbon—another violation of modern radio protocol—and feel the warmth spread through my chest. Outside the booth windows, Boston glitters in the October darkness, all glass towers and gentrification where dive bars used to breed bands.
"But tonight, I want to tell you about the night I watched the future of rock and roll die in a high school cafeteria. March fifteenth, 1979. Two kids from opposite sides of a brook that might as well have been the Atlantic Ocean."
The console lights blink expectantly. I've got carts loaded with Aerosmith's "Dream On," Queen's "Bohemian Rhapsody," The Cars, The J. Geils Band, all the legends. But first, I need to take you back to when those legends were just beginning to cast shadows.
"Sudbury, Massachusetts—twenty miles west of here for you newcomers. The town where revolution was born, zip code 01776, but they'd perfected the art of keeping their own people in separate corners. Hop Brook ran through the middle like God had drawn a property line, separating those who worried about mortgage payments from those who worried about mortgage rates."
I spin my chair to face the wall of albums, each one a memory, a story, a moment when music mattered more than demographics. My fingers find the right cart without looking—muscle memory from five decades of curating sound.
"Nick Johnson lived where the mortgage was always late and dreams were luxuries you couldn't afford. His father Dan had died in a construction accident two years earlier—steel beams and a snapped cable, the kind of industrial tragedy that makes page three and gets forgotten by everyone except the families who eat silence for dinner. His mother Grace worked doubles at Friendly's, came home smelling like fryer oil and other people's happiness."
The bourbon burns just right. In the engineering booth, the kid they've assigned to babysit my final show looks up from his phone. Maybe something in my voice warns him this won't be the nostalgia trip he expected.
"Jackie Marsh lived less than a mile away as the crow flies, but between them stretched everything America uses to keep its children in their proper places. His father taught economics at Boston University, wrote papers about the working class without ever living in their neighborhoods. Jackie's basement was worth more than the Johnson house. He had a voice trained by teachers who charged more per lesson than Grace made in a week."
I pause, letting the silence breathe. Radio silence used to terrify program directors, but I learned long ago that the spaces between words can say more than the words themselves.
"These boys should never have met. But music has always been democracy's wild card, the thing that ignores the boundaries we build."
Through the booth window, I can see the billboard across Boylston Street advertising luxury condos where The Rat used to host bands too raw for radio. Twenty-four hundred square feet starting at 2.2 million. The Ramones played that club in '77. Joey was so tall he had to duck under the pipes backstage.
"I met them both the night of their battle. Not a battle of the bands—that makes it sound like a game. This was a war for which America they were going to inherit."
I load the first song—not one of the hits, but a scratchy demo that never made it to vinyl. The levels need adjusting; the left channel distorts if you push it past six. But that imperfection is the point.
"This is the only recording that exists of Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh playing together. Ninety-three seconds from a summer camp in 1974, when they were thirteen and still believed harmony was possible between kids from different sides of anything. Listen to it. Listen to what we lost."
The demo starts—two young voices finding harmony over acoustic guitars, neither Aerosmith nor Queen but something uniquely theirs. It lasts ninety-three seconds before cutting off mid-verse. That's all that survived.
"Ninety-three seconds of proof that we chose division over music. The rest of the story? That's what these final hours are for."
The ON AIR sign pulses like a heartbeat. I crack my knuckles—arthritis now, too many decades of spinning vinyl—and lean into the microphone.
"But first, we need to talk about a garage on Dutton Road, and why the ghost of a dead construction worker might have been the only adult who understood what was really at stake..."



Chapter 2A: The Geography of Division
Picture Sudbury from above in March 1979, and you'll see America in miniature—a town carved into quarters like a medieval map of the humors, each section believing it represents the real America while the others are somehow less authentic.
Hop Brook runs north to south through the center, named for the hops that fed the old mill before progress declared agriculture inefficient. The brook starts in Ashland, gathers momentum through Sudbury, and dies in the Sudbury River near the Wayside Inn where Henry Ford tried to preserve American authenticity by buying it wholesale. Even our water has a sense of irony.
But Hop Brook is only half the story. Running east to west, bisecting the town at a perfect right angle, sits what everyone simply called "the Dump"—twenty-three acres of American excess where Saturday mornings taught democracy and disposal in equal measure. The official name was Sudbury Sanitary Landfill, but nobody used official names for places that smelled like truth.
These two lines—water and waste—created four distinct Sudburys, each with its own ZIP code anxiety, its own relationship to the American dream, its own soundtrack.
Northeast: The working-class quarter that bordered Lincoln—a town so separate that most Sudbury residents would never set foot there in their lifetime, though it shared the regional high school. Lincoln existed like a ghost town just a football toss away, known to Sudburyites only through the hyphen in "Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School." Row houses and triple-deckers lined streets named after trees that had been cut down to build them—Maple, Oak, Elm. This was where fathers worked construction and overtime, where mothers counted coupons and prayed the car would pass inspection. Kids here learned guitar because instruments were cheaper than therapy, and both were cheaper than expecting college.
Southeast: The rural quarter, where the Cavicchio pig farm sprawled against the landfill in what seemed like cosmic joke but was actually cosmic justice. Three hundred acres of pigs eating garbage, garbage feeding pigs, the circle of life with a Boston accent. The smell when the wind was wrong could peel paint off houses a mile away. Giuseppe Cavicchio had refused to sell his water rights to Henry Ford in 1923, blocking Ford's plan for an auto parts factory. Ford called it "Italian stubbornness." Giuseppe called it "understanding what land is worth."
Southwest: The forest quarter, protected by the Sudbury Valley Trustees and careful zoning that preserved both trees and property values. Colonial homes with original wide-plank floors sat on lots measured in acres rather than feet. This was where professors lived, where mothers ran the PTA with military efficiency, where kids learned piano because musical literacy was assumed, not earned.
Northwest: The money quarter, where the Revolutionary War seemed to have ended just yesterday and on favorable terms. Stone walls older than the Constitution marked boundaries between estates named after dead Indians and living WASPS. These houses had names, not just addresses. The kids here didn't learn instruments—they took lessons, with the assumption that talent could be purchased like everything else worth having.
Between these four quarters ran the transportation lines that proved connection wasn't the same as unity. The Massachusetts Central Railroad ran east to west, carrying freight that never stopped in Sudbury because Sudbury had nothing to export except tax revenue. The Framingham and Lowell Railroad ran north to south, parallel to Hop Brook, carrying commuters to Boston jobs that paid for Sudbury dreams.
Where these lines crossed at South Sudbury station, engineers called it a "diamond crossing"—four tracks meeting at perfect angles, each train required to stop and wait for the other to pass. It was the perfect metaphor for a town where paths crossed but never really connected.
By 1979, both railroad lines were failing, killed by highways and the American love affair with individual transportation. The diamond crossing still functioned, but barely, like an old man's heart—still beating but not with conviction.
The Dump operated on a democratic principle that would have made Jefferson proud if he could have held his nose. Every Saturday morning, families from all four quarters lined up in their cars—station wagons from the money quarter, pickup trucks from Lincoln, rusted sedans from the forest edges where professors lived on academic salaries, and the Cavicchios' truck that always smelled like pig and carried twice as much as anyone else's.
At the Dump, ZIP codes didn't matter. Everyone's garbage stank equally. Everyone's children learned the same lesson about American consumption: we create more waste than we can hide, but burial is always an option.
The kids would sit in the cars while their fathers—always their fathers, this was 1979—would back up to the edge of the pit where bulldozers waited like mechanical vultures. Glass in one area, paper in another, everything else into the maw that would be covered with six inches of dirt and forgotten until the EPA decided forgetting wasn't an option anymore.
Between the Dump and the pig farm ran a network of dirt roads that connected to nothing official but everything important. This was where the mini-bikes ruled—Honda 50s and Yamaha 60s, ridden by kids who understood that freedom required small engines and large territories. The trails they carved would outlast the bikes, outlast the kids, outlast everything except the earth's memory of where children once believed they could go anywhere.
These were the borders that mattered in March 1979, when two boys who lived less than a mile apart discovered that distance isn't measured in feet but in expectations, opportunities, and the particular American talent for maintaining divisions while preaching unity.
The town center sat like a colonial film set, complete with white churches, a green where sheep once grazed, and the stocks where colonial justice once embarrassed sin into submission. By 1979, the stocks were gone but the impulse remained—just more subtle, more efficient at keeping people in their proper places.
This was the geography that would shape a Battle of the Bands, that would turn a music competition into a class war, that would prove—once again—that in America, even harmony has to fight for its right to exist.
And somewhere in this divided landscape, three kids who'd once made music together were about to remember what they'd forgotten, or forget what they'd remembered, depending on which side of which line they'd learned to call home.

Chapter 2: The Garage That Held More Than Cars (Expanded)
I cue up Aerosmith's "Dream On" from their first album and let it play while I gather my thoughts. The bourbon catches the light from the console LEDs, amber in a glass older than my babysitting engineer.
"A garage," I say on air, "can be a temple if you know how to worship."
I'd driven out to Dutton Road three weeks before the Battle of the Bands, my powder-blue Karmann Ghia looking ridiculous navigating the potholes that the town somehow never fixed on the working-class side of Hop Brook. But I'd heard rumors. A kid who made Steven Tyler sound like he was holding back. A garage where the sound hit you in the chest before you even opened the door.
The Johnson house squatted like it was tired: peeling paint the color of old mustard, sagging gutters that had given up pretending to channel water properly, front steps that listed to port like a ship taking on water slowly, gracefully, inevitably. The lawn had surrendered to New England winter months ago, brown grass poking through dirty snow like stubble on a dead man's face. A single window glowed yellow in the kitchen, where Grace Johnson was probably sitting at the Formica table, still in her Friendly's uniform, calculating whether the heat could stay on until next week's paycheck.
But from the detached garage came something alive—distorted power chords that made my radio-trained ears perk up like a dog hearing its master's key in the lock.
"Mind if I listen?" I'd asked Grace Johnson when she answered the door after three knocks, wiping her hands on a dish towel that had seen better decades. She was still in her Friendly's uniform, the polyester blend that made everyone look like they were melting slowly under fluorescent lights. The name tag read GRACE in that cheerful corporate font that lies about everything—how much you matter, how much you're worth, how much dignity comes with asking if someone wants fries with that.
She studied me—this Boston radio personality in bell-bottoms and oversized sunglasses, standing on her cracked front step like some visitor from a planet where people had money for clothes that didn't come from Jordan Marsh clearance racks. "You're that lady from WBCN. The one who broke Aerosmith."
"Guilty as charged."
"Nick's in the garage." She paused, and I saw something flicker across her face—hope, maybe, or fear that hope was dangerous, expensive, something they couldn't afford to budget for. "Don't fill his head with dreams we can't afford."
The warning hung in the March air between us like visible breath, each word carrying the weight of a woman who'd learned that dreams were luxuries that came after rent, groceries, car repairs, and all the other mathematics of survival that the middle class never had to calculate to the penny.
Back in the booth, 2024, I watch my engineer scroll through his phone, probably checking his stock portfolio or whatever twenty-eight-year-olds check when they think nobody's watching them avoid work. He has no idea what a garage means to rock and roll. He grew up with GarageBand, thinking music could be manufactured in a bedroom with the right plugins and enough Auto-Tune to make a dying cat sound like Whitney Houston. He never felt the sacred geometry of four walls, concrete floor, and kids who had nothing to lose except the pain they were trying to turn into something bearable.
"The thing about Nick Johnson's garage," I tell my invisible audience, lighting another cigarette because who's going to fire me now, "was that it remembered his father."
Dan Johnson had died on November fourteenth, 1977, a Tuesday morning so ordinary that nobody saw tragedy coming until it arrived with sirens and flashing lights. He was working the Prudential Tower project, thirty-seven stories of Boston ambition rising from the Back Bay like a middle finger pointed at the sky. Dan ran a crane, one of those massive yellow beasts that could lift ten tons of steel beam like it was picking up a pencil.
The cable snapped at 10:17 AM. OSHA would later determine that the cable had been scheduled for replacement two weeks earlier, but the general contractor had delayed the order to save money on a project already running over budget. The load—four steel I-beams destined for the thirty-second floor—fell like God's judgment onto the construction trailer where Dan had been checking blueprints, eating a tuna sandwich Grace had made that morning, probably thinking about Nick's algebra grades or the mortgage payment due the following week.
They said he died instantly, which is what they always tell the families because instant death is supposed to be a mercy, as if the speed of dying somehow makes up for the fact of it. Grace never believed them. She knew Dan well enough to know he would have fought it, would have tried to dive under the desk or behind the file cabinet, would have spent his last seconds thinking about getting home to his wife and seventeen-year-old son who was learning guitar in the garage Dan had built with his own hands.
The construction company paid the minimum required by law—$50,000, which sounds like a fortune until you realize it has to last the rest of two lives. The insurance company fought the claim for eight months, arguing that Dan had violated safety protocols by being in the trailer during a lift operation, as if death wasn't punishment enough without adding blame to grief.
Grace had to go back to work three weeks after the funeral. Friendly's gave her two weeks bereavement leave, which was considered generous for a part-time employee, and then she was back behind the counter asking if people wanted to make their sundae a full-size for just fifty cents more, smiling because tips depended on smiles and tips made the difference between keeping the house and moving to the kind of apartment complex where thin walls carried the sound of other people's failures.
His tools still hung on the pegboard in the garage, arranged with the precision of a man who understood that organization was the difference between efficiency and chaos, between getting home in time for dinner and working overtime you couldn't afford to turn down. Hammers arranged by size, from the delicate eight-ounce finish hammer to the four-pound sledge that could drive fence posts or demolish small worlds. Wrenches in descending order, each one hanging exactly where Dan's muscle memory expected to find it. The socket set was missing the 9/16ths—he'd had it in his pocket that Tuesday morning, probably planning to tighten something when he got home, some small domestic repair that would never happen now.
The garage smelled like motor oil and sawdust, WD-40 and the ghost of Dan's Old Spice aftershave. It was twenty feet by twenty feet of American masculinity, the kind of space where fathers taught sons about the dignity of working with your hands, about the difference between the right tool and the good-enough tool, about how to fix things instead of throwing them away and buying new ones.
When I entered the garage that March afternoon, Nick was mid-solo, his fingers finding notes that shouldn't have been possible on a guitar held together with electrical tape and determination. The instrument had cost twenty-five dollars at a pawn shop in Framingham, the kind of place where dreams go to die and occasionally get resurrected by teenagers with lawn-mowing money and more hope than sense.
Three other kids backed him—all from Lincoln, that ghost town just beyond the northeast border that most Sudbury kids pretended didn't exist. Stephen Murphy, David Cassel, Mark Pianka—inseparable since elementary school, musical outcasts who'd found in Nick the fourth corner of a square they didn't know they were building. Stephen on bass, tall and thin like John Entwistle if Entwistle had grown up on food stamps and hand-me-downs. David behind a drum kit assembled from three different sets, none matching but all thunderous. Mark on keyboards, fingers doing all his talking on a Casio his grandmother had given him for Christmas when he was twelve.
They were social pariahs at Lincoln-Sudbury, the Lincoln boys who rode the bus from the forgotten town, who ate lunch by themselves, who'd never been invited to parties they couldn't afford to attend anyway. But put instruments in their hands and they became something else—a rhythm section that locked together like they shared the same heartbeat, like they'd been born to make music no one else wanted to hear.
They stopped when they saw me, that teenage terror of being caught in the act of caring about something, of revealing that beneath the carefully constructed armor of not-giving-a-shit was a desperate need to matter, to be heard, to prove that their lives meant something more than the statistics that said kids like them didn't make it out.
"Don't stop on my account," I said, settling onto a paint can in the corner, breathing in the mixture of motor oil and teenage ambition that made the garage smell like possibility and limitation in equal measure. "Play it like you mean it."
They exchanged glances—four working-class boys deciding whether to trust this intruder from the world of actual radio, where records got played and careers got made and people like them occasionally escaped the gravity of their ZIP codes. Nick made the decision for them, launching back into "Dream On" with a fury that transformed the garage into something larger than its concrete dimensions.
Here's what I need you to understand about authenticity: it's not something you can learn at Berklee College of Music, where rich kids go to study the blues they've never lived. It's not in perfect pitch or precision fingering or the kind of technical skill that comes from expensive lessons and patient teachers. It's in the way Nick Johnson's voice cracked when he hit the high notes, not from poor technique but from feeling too much to contain, from trying to compress seventeen years of grief and rage and love into three minutes of sound.
It's in the way his hands moved on the fretboard like they were trying to pull music out of the wood itself, like the guitar was a divining rod searching for water in a desert of broken dreams and deferred hopes. It's in the way he sang "Every time that I look in the mirror, all these lines on my face getting clearer" and made you believe that seventeen years could feel like seventy when you've been the man of the house since fifteen, when you've watched your mother cry over bills when she thought you weren't looking, when you've learned that dreaming was something you did quietly so nobody could take it away from you.
When they finished, the silence in that garage was holy. Not church holy—none of us were the church type—but sacred in the way that only truth can be sacred, the kind of quiet that happens after something real has been spoken in a world full of lies.
"What's your band called?" I asked, my voice rougher than I'd expected.
Nick shrugged, that teenage gesture that protects against caring too much, against revealing how desperately you want something you're probably not going to get. "Don't have a name yet. We're just... playing."
"You're doing more than playing," I said, standing up from the paint can, my knees protesting because concrete floors are unforgiving even to thirty-five-year-old knees that think they're still young. "You got a demo?"
The other boys looked at Nick like he was the designated spokesperson for their collective dream. He shook his head. "Can't afford studio time."
I pulled out my card—back when WBCN business cards meant something, when showing one could open doors instead of starting conversations about the decline of traditional media and the rise of streaming services and how everything was different now and mostly worse.
"Come by the station. After midnight, when the suits are gone and the real work gets done. We'll lay down some tracks, see what you sound like when you're not competing with the ghost of a dead man's tools."
The hope on those boys' faces could have powered half of Boston. The kind of hope that hurts to look at directly, like staring at the sun, because you know how rare it is and how quickly it can be extinguished by the thousand small cruelties that America visits on kids who dare to believe they might matter.
But Nick hesitated, and in that hesitation was everything his mother had taught him about being careful, about not reaching for things that were too high, too far, too expensive for people like them.
"Why?" he asked, and his voice carried the wariness of someone who'd learned that adults offering opportunities usually wanted something in return. "Why would you help us?"
I looked around that garage—at Dan Johnson's tools hanging like relics of a more hopeful time, at the oil stains on the concrete floor that told the story of a thousand small repairs, at the four boys holding instruments like life preservers in an ocean of limited options.
"Because rock and roll is supposed to be for kids like you," I said, meaning every word. "Kids who play in garages because concert halls don't know they exist yet. Kids who understand that music isn't decoration—it's survival. Because your father's tools on that pegboard know more about honest work than half the industry executives in this city."
I paused at the garage door, looking back at four boys who were trying not to hope too hard, trying not to believe that this moment might be the one that changed everything.
"And because Steven Tyler played his first gig in a garage not much bigger than this one. The only difference between him and you is someone opened a door."
But here's the thing about doors—I thought I was opening one for Nick Johnson and his band of working-class dreamers. I didn't know I was also closing another, somewhere across Hop Brook, where a different boy was struggling with his own kind of prison, his own need to be heard, his own desperate desire to turn pain into music and music into meaning.
Sometimes the cruelest thing about opportunity is how little of it there is to go around.

Chapter 3: The Gilded Cage (Expanded)
"Now," I continue, cueing up Queen's "Under Pressure" because some stories require their own soundtrack, "let me tell you about the prince in the tower."
The same week I discovered Nick Johnson's garage temple, I received a phone call that would complicate everything. Dr. Richard Marsh had called the station personally—not his secretary, not his assistant, but the man himself, which should have been my first warning that this wasn't just another proud parent pushing his prodigy.
"Ms. Sartori," his voice carried that particular Boston Brahmin accent that turns 'car' into 'cah' but somehow makes it sound aristocratic rather than provincial. "I understand you have an ear for... emerging talent."
"I have an ear for music," I'd corrected, because I'd learned to be wary of people who talked around what they meant.
"Precisely. My son Jackie is having a recital next week. He's been accepted to Juilliard's pre-college program—full scholarship, though naturally we don't need it. But before he leaves for New York, we thought... that is, he thought... perhaps someone from the local music community should witness what serious training can accomplish."
The pause after "serious training" lasted just long enough to make clear what he thought of garage bands and radio DJs and anyone who confused volume with virtue.
"I don't usually cover classical recitals," I'd said, but something in his voice—a father's pride mixed with something else, something that sounded almost like fear—made me curious.
"It's not entirely classical," Dr. Marsh had admitted, and in that admission was the crack in his carefully constructed world. "Jackie has been... experimenting. With different genres. We're hoping exposure to genuine musical authority might help him refocus on what matters."
Translation: my son is rebelling and I need an adult to tell him he's being foolish.
The Marsh house stood on Concord Road like it had been placed there by a decorator with unlimited budget and limited imagination. Colonial perfection in white clapboard and black shutters, each window box planted with flowers that bloomed on schedule, lawn so green it looked painted. The circular driveway could have hosted a small parade, and probably had during Jackie's elaborate birthday parties that the northeast quarter kids heard about but were never invited to attend.
Walking up to that front door—mahogany thick enough to stop bullets, brass knocker polished to mirror brightness—was like approaching a museum exhibit titled "How the Other Half Lives, Circa 1979." Even the doorbell played a classical melody, probably Bach, definitely pretentious.
Mrs. Eileen Marsh answered the door herself, which surprised me until I remembered that Wednesday was probably the help's day off. She was beautiful in that carefully maintained way that money could purchase—hair colored by professionals, skin preserved by expensive creams, clothes that whispered rather than shouted their cost. She moved like a dancer who'd never forgotten her training, every gesture calculated for maximum grace and minimum effort.
"Ms. Sartori, welcome. Richard is so pleased you could come." Her smile was perfect, practiced, the kind developed at boarding schools where charm was considered a life skill. "We've prepared the living room as a concert space. I do hope you'll find it adequate."
Adequate. The living room had been transformed into something that rivaled Symphony Hall. Persian rugs worth more than cars, oil paintings that belonged in museums, furniture that had probably been sitting in the same spots since the Adams administration. Folding chairs arranged in perfect rows faced a rented Steinway grand that cost more than Grace Johnson would make in five years. Champagne chilled in silver buckets, hors d'oeuvres arranged on china that had been in the family since someone important had dinner with someone else important sometime when America was still figuring out what it wanted to be.
The audience was exactly what you'd expect: prep school parents and university faculty, summer house owners and trust fund guardians, the kind of people who considered themselves cultured because they could pronounce "Tchaikovsky" correctly and owned first edition books they'd never actually read. They applauded with their fingertips and discussed their latest European vacations between movements, treating music like expensive wallpaper—pleasant background for more important conversations about stock portfolios and boarding school admissions.
Jackie entered wearing a tuxedo that fit like it had been grown on him rather than tailored. Seventeen years old but carrying himself like someone who'd been preparing for this moment since birth, every step measured, every gesture the product of deportment lessons and etiquette classes and the kind of training that taught you how to present yourself to people who mattered. He was beautiful in that way that good nutrition and orthodontics and personal trainers could manufacture—tall, aristocratic, blessed with the kind of bone structure that made photographers weep with joy.
But when he sat at the piano, something shifted. The performance mask slipped just enough to reveal something real underneath—a boy who'd spent so many years being told what excellence looked like that he'd forgotten what it felt like.
He opened with Chopin's Nocturne in E-flat major, because of course he did. The piece that every piano student tackles eventually, the musical equivalent of climbing Mount Washington—challenging enough to impress, familiar enough to judge. His technique was flawless, every note struck with precision that spoke of thousands of hours of practice, of lessons that started before memory, of parents who considered anything less than perfection a character flaw.
The audience nodded appreciatively, their champagne glasses reflecting the chandelier light like tiny crystal prisons. They were hearing what they expected to hear—technical mastery, cultural sophistication, proof that their investment in proper education was paying dividends. Dr. Marsh stood in the back of the room, arms crossed, face wearing the expression of a man whose plans were proceeding exactly as designed.
But I was watching Jackie's face, and I saw something his parents had trained themselves not to notice: he was dying in there. Behind the technical perfection was a boy playing someone else's definition of music, speaking someone else's language with flawless pronunciation but no soul, no personal investment, no sense that these notes meant anything beyond their market value in the cultural stock exchange.
He moved through the program like a beautiful robot: Bach's Goldberg Variations (selected excerpts, naturally), Debussy's "Clair de Lune" (because someone had told him it was romantic), Rachmaninoff's Prelude in C-sharp minor (because difficulty impressed people who confused complexity with depth). Each piece performed with technical excellence and emotional vacancy, like watching a surgeon operate on a mannequin.
Then something happened. Between the Rachmaninoff and what was supposed to be the evening's finale—Mozart's Sonata in A major, K. 331—Jackie stopped. Just sat there at the piano, hands in his lap, staring at the keys like they might tell him something important.
The audience stirred uncomfortably. This wasn't in the program. Spontaneity was not part of the evening's carefully choreographed demonstration of cultural superiority.
Dr. Marsh cleared his throat—a warning about maintaining proper recital pacing, about not disappointing the guests who had graciously donated their Wednesday evening to witness his son's excellence.
But Jackie wasn't listening to his father. He was listening to something else, something that had been trying to break through years of classical conditioning like water finding cracks in a dam.
His fingers found different keys. The opening piano chords of "Under Pressure"—that David Bowie and Queen collaboration that sounded like anxiety having a nervous breakdown—began to emerge from the Steinway like contraband smuggled into a church service.
The room froze. You could hear pearls clutching themselves, champagne glasses stopping halfway to carefully lipsticked mouths, the collective intake of breath from people who'd never been challenged by anything more dangerous than choosing between brie and camembert.
"Pressure, pushing down on me," Jackie sang, his classically trained voice doing things that Freddie Mercury would have envied, "Pressing down on you, no man ask for..."
And here's what that room full of cultivated taste and purchased refinement didn't understand: Jackie Marsh wasn't defiling their temple. He was consecrating it. He was taking their precious Steinway and making it confess that rock and roll was just classical music that had learned to feel something, that had permission to break its own rules, that understood emotion was more important than technique.
For four minutes and twenty-nine seconds, Jackie Marsh was free. His hands found chord progressions that shouldn't have been possible on a solo piano, harmonies that transformed Queen's anthem of anxiety into something that sounded like prayer, like confession, like a seventeen-year-old boy admitting he was suffocating in silk and cashmere and the weight of other people's expectations.
"Under pressure that burns a building down," he sang, and every word was autobiography, every note was rebellion, every chord change was a declaration that he was more than the sum of his expensive parts.
When he finished, the silence was different from any of the other silences that evening. This wasn't the appreciative quiet that followed technical mastery. This was the silence of scandal, of propriety interrupted, of carefully maintained facades cracking just enough to reveal the chaos underneath.
Dr. Marsh was the first to speak, his voice carrying the controlled fury of a man whose plans had been publicly derailed by his own son. "That was... unexpected."
"That was rock and roll," I said, standing up because someone needed to, because I'd just witnessed something that mattered more than all the Chopin nocturnes in the world. "That was your son showing you who he really is."
Jackie looked at me then, really looked, and I saw the same desperate hunger I'd seen in Nick Johnson's eyes—a need to be heard as himself, not as someone's projection of who he should be, not as a trophy to be displayed or an investment to be protected, but as a seventeen-year-old boy who'd discovered that music could be salvation if you were brave enough to let it.
"You're Maxanne Sartori," he said, his voice carrying wonder like he'd just recognized a celebrity, except the celebrity was someone who might actually understand what he was trying to say. "You play music that matters."
Music that matters. Not the program music his parents approved of, not the cultural vegetables he was supposed to consume for his own good, but music that felt like life instead of homework.
"I play music," I corrected gently. "Sometimes it comes from famous bands. Sometimes it comes from kids in garages. And sometimes," I looked directly at Dr. Marsh, letting him understand exactly what I thought of his careful plans, "it comes from piano prodigies who understand that Chopin would have loved electric guitars if he'd lived to see them."
The evening ended quickly after that. The champagne suddenly tasted flat, the hors d'oeuvres lost their appeal, and the audience began making those polite excuses that wealthy people had perfected—early morning meetings, babysitter schedules, sudden headaches that required immediate departure.
But before I left, I gave Jackie my card—the same one I'd given Nick Johnson three days earlier, though I didn't mention that connection because some ironies are too complicated to explain in a living room full of people who wouldn't understand them anyway.
"Come by the station," I told him quietly while his parents said goodbye to guests and pretended the evening had gone according to plan. "Bring whatever music lives in you, not what lives in your parents' expectations."
Later, driving back through Sudbury toward Boston, I thought about what I'd witnessed. Two boys on opposite sides of Hop Brook, both prisoners of expectations—one expected to fail, the other expected to succeed, both trapped by predictions that left no room for who they actually were or what they might become if given the chance to choose their own cages.
But here's the cruelest irony of all: I thought I was saving two boys from the boxes they'd been born into. I didn't realize I was setting them on a collision course that would prove boxes are easier to escape than each other, that sometimes the cruelest prison is the one that looks like freedom, that sounds like music, that promises to rescue you from everything except the thing that matters most—the distance between who you are and who you're supposed to be.
Back in 2024, I drain another glass of water and think about Jackie Marsh at seventeen, playing "Under Pressure" in his parents' living room like his life depended on it. Which, in a way, it did. Because that was the last time he would play music like it mattered more than approval, more than success, more than the careful preservation of advantages he'd never asked for but couldn't afford to lose.
Sometimes the most dangerous thing you can do is show people who you really are. And sometimes the most tragic thing is discovering that being yourself is a luxury you can't afford, especially when everyone around you has invested so heavily in the person they think you should be.

Chapter 4A: Summer 1974 - When Harmony Was Possible
"But before I tell you about the war," I say, cueing up James Taylor's "Fire and Rain" because sometimes you need someone who understands Massachusetts sadness, "I need to tell you about the peace. About two weeks in the summer of 1974 when three thirteen-year-olds discovered that music could be bigger than the accidents of where you were born."
Camp Winaukee sat on the shores of Lake Winnisquam in New Hampshire like a postcard from America's innocent past. Rustic cabins that smelled like pine sap and teenage anxiety, a dining hall where food fights were democracy in action, and most importantly for our story, a music building where kids from different worlds could find common ground in common time signatures.
The camp wasn't exclusively musical—there was swimming, hiking, pottery for kids whose parents thought art therapy might straighten them out. But the music program drew a particular type of kid: the ones who'd rather practice than play capture the flag, who carried instruments like other kids carried comic books, who understood that noise could be shaped into meaning if you just worked hard enough and wanted it badly enough.
Nick Johnson was there on scholarship—one of those programs designed to make rich donors feel good about their tax-deductible charity. The paperwork had taken Grace six months to complete, forms that asked questions about family income and father's occupation with the kind of precision that made poverty feel like a moral failing. Dan Johnson had signed the forms with hands stained from construction work, proud that his son might have opportunities he'd never imagined.
"Two weeks," Dan had said, looking at the camp brochure with its pictures of smiling kids around campfires and music rooms that looked like small concert halls. "Two weeks of not worrying about anything except learning to play."
Nick had packed his guitar—the pawn shop special with the warped neck and the buzz on the third fret—in a case held together with electrician's tape and hope. Grace had sewn name tags into every piece of clothing he owned, as if losing a t-shirt at summer camp would somehow matter more than losing everything else they couldn't afford to replace.
Jackie Marsh arrived in a BMW driven by parents who'd made this decision over dinner at the country club, consulting with other parents whose children were also being invested in rather than simply raised. His guitar was a Martin D-28, the kind of instrument that professional musicians saved for years to afford, bought for him because Dr. Marsh believed that excellence required excellent tools.
"The difference between Nick and Jackie that first day," I tell my audience, "wasn't just money. It was assumptions. Nick arrived believing he was lucky to be there. Jackie arrived believing he deserved to be there. Both assumptions were wrong, but in different ways."
They met in Cabin 7, the music intensive cabin where kids who'd chosen instruments over athletics were quartered together like some experiment in cultural diversity. Eight boys from different corners of New England, thrown together for two weeks of forced harmony.
The first week was careful territory-mapping. Nick gravitating toward the kids whose clothes came from K-Mart and whose instruments showed wear. Jackie finding the prep school boys whose parents had enrolled them to add "musical accomplishment" to college applications that were already being planned for children who'd barely reached adolescence.
They didn't speak, didn't acknowledge each other, maintained the careful distance that thirteen-year-olds perfect when they know they're different but don't yet understand why difference has to matter.
Then Patty Lally arrived.
She was in the girls' cabin across the clearing, but sound travels across water, and during the evening practice sessions, her voice drifted through the screens like a promise that music could be simple, honest, real. Not trained like Jackie's voice, not raw like Nick's, but something in between—human, imperfect, true in a way that made everyone else sound like they were lying.
"Patty wasn't the prettiest girl at camp," I continue, "but she was the most magnetic. Wheat-colored hair that caught sunlight like it was storing it for later, eyes that actually listened when you talked, and a way of moving through the world like she belonged wherever she happened to be standing."
Both boys noticed. Of course they did. They were thirteen, hormones were deciding their daily moods, and here was a girl who could sing like angels would sing if angels had been raised in small New England towns by grandmothers who remembered when music was something you made, not something you consumed.
Nick would find excuses to walk past the girls' cabin during free periods, hoping to catch her practicing. Jackie would linger after meals, positioning himself where he could watch her laugh with her cabin mates, studying her like she was a piece of music he was trying to learn to play.
It was Patty who brought them together, accidentally and inevitably.
Week two, Thursday evening practice session. She was sitting alone on the dock, guitar in her lap, working through a song she'd written about homesickness. Nick had crept to the edge of the clearing to listen, hidden behind a white pine that smelled like childhood and summer and the particular sadness of knowing good things end. Jackie had positioned himself on the porch of the music building, close enough to hear but far enough away to maintain plausible deniability about eavesdropping.
"You can come closer," Patty had called without looking up from her guitar. "Both of you. Music sounds better when it's shared."
They approached from different directions like dogs who'd been called by the same master, Nick from the woods, Jackie from the building, both trying to look casual while their hearts hammered against their ribs.
"I know you," Patty said to Nick. "You're the scholarship kid with the guitar that sounds like it's in pain."
Nick's face reddened. Being the scholarship kid wasn't something you announced—it was something you carried like a scar you hoped people wouldn't notice.
"And you," she turned to Jackie, "are the rich kid who plays like he's afraid of making mistakes."
Jackie's face went through several expressions before settling on surprised. Nobody had ever called him afraid before. Careful, precise, dedicated—but never afraid.
"Want to try something?" Patty asked, patting the dock on either side of her. "I've got a song that needs more than one voice."
They sat—Nick to her left, Jackie to her right, the girl in the middle like Switzerland mediating between countries that hadn't declared war yet. The dock smelled like lake water and creosote, teenage sunscreen and the particular anxiety of kids who were about to try something that might fail spectacularly.
"It's called 'Common Ground,'" Patty said, tuning her guitar with the confidence of someone who'd been doing it since she could hold a pick. "It's about this place where all the rivers meet, where the water doesn't care about ZIP codes."
She started playing, just three chords and a melody that sounded like it had always existed, like she'd discovered it rather than written it. Her voice was clear, honest, unafraid:
"There's a place where the water runs shallow, Where the kids from everywhere come to play..."
Nick found the harmony first—his voice sliding underneath hers like it had been looking for that exact space, like coming home to a house you'd never seen but always known. Jackie listened, found the space above both of them, his trained voice adding something that made the simple song suddenly complex, beautiful, complete.
For two minutes and forty-seven seconds—I timed it later, when I was trying to understand how long harmony could last before the world interrupted—they were perfect together. Three kids from different towns, different backgrounds, different futures, finding a sound that none of them could make alone.
When they finished, the lake lapped against the dock pilings like applause. Across the water, kids from other cabins had stopped their own activities to listen, drawn by something they couldn't name but recognized as important.
"Wow," Nick said, and it was the first word he'd spoken to Jackie since arriving at camp.
"Yeah," Jackie agreed, and it was the first time he'd sounded like a thirteen-year-old instead of a small adult.
"You want to try another one?" Patty asked, and the smile she gave them both was democracy in action—equal attention, equal warmth, equal possibility.
They spent the next three days writing songs together. Not covers of famous bands, not imitations of their heroes, but original music that told the story of who they were when nobody was watching, when expectations couldn't reach them, when being thirteen was enough of an accomplishment.
"The song about the railroad crossing," Nick suggested during their last practice session. They were in the music building after lights-out, technically violating camp rules but protected by counselors who understood that some learning happened after official hours.
"Where all the trains have to stop," Jackie added, his classical training helping him find chord progressions that supported Nick's melody.
"And wait for each other to pass," Patty finished, because she understood metaphors instinctively, the way some people understand languages or mathematics or the precise moment when summer starts turning into fall.
They called it "Diamond Crossing"—about a place where paths met but didn't connect, where movement was possible but only in predetermined directions, where everything and nothing could happen at the same intersection.
"But here's what killed me," I tell my 2024 audience, lighting another cigarette with hands that remember that summer too well, "watching those three kids together. Because you could see it happening in real time—Nick and Jackie competing for Patty's attention in the way that thirteen-year-old boys compete for everything: who could make her laugh, who could suggest better chord changes, who could sit closer during practice without making it obvious they were trying to sit closer."
It wasn't love—they were too young for that particular devastation. It was something simpler and more complicated: the recognition that here was someone worth impressing, worth trying to be better for, worth the risk of caring about.
"I like you both," Patty had said one evening when their competitive dynamic was becoming too obvious to ignore. "But I like you better when you're playing together than when you're trying to outplay each other."
The last night of camp, they performed "Common Ground" for the whole camp. Three kids on a makeshift stage, guitars and voices finding harmonies that proved music could be democracy when nobody was keeping score, when winning wasn't the point, when the only audience that mattered was yourself and the people brave enough to make noise with you.
The applause went on forever—or at least long enough for three thirteen-year-olds to believe that this was how life would always be, that music would always matter more than money, that harmony was their natural state and discord was just something that happened to other people.
They exchanged addresses, promised to write, swore they'd form a band when they got home. Made the kinds of promises that thirteen-year-olds make when summer is ending and the real world is waiting like a patient predator.
But here's the thing about addresses: they're not just locations. They're destinies. Nick's address was Dutton Road, Sudbury, where the mortgage was always late and dreams were budgeted last. Jackie's was Concord Road, where success was expected and failure was unthinkable. Patty's was in Concord proper, where revolution had been born and then museumified into something safe for tourists.
Those addresses contained everything—school districts and property values, opportunities and limitations, the particular American genius for making geography into destiny.
"They wrote letters," I continue, "for about six weeks. Then school started, and school reminded them who they were supposed to be. Nick learned that kids from Concord Road had different cafeteria tables, different expectations, different futures. Jackie learned that scholarship kids spoke a language he'd never been taught, carried experiences he'd never been allowed to have, understood things about survival that Juilliard prep courses didn't cover."
By Christmas, the letters had stopped. By spring, they passed each other in the hallways of Lincoln-Sudbury like strangers who might have shared a dream once but couldn't quite remember the details.
The camp closed permanently in 1976, a victim of insurance costs and the liability culture that was slowly strangling every institution that allowed children to take actual risks, to fail authentically, to discover who they were when nobody was managing their development.
"But that dock is still there," I say, stubbing out my cigarette with unnecessary force. "Lake Winnisquam, New Hampshire. If you know where to look, you can still see the scuff marks where three kids sat and made music that proved harmony was possible between any two voices if you just listened hard enough to find the space where they could coexist."
I check the clock—not for Maxanne's countdown, but because time always matters when you're telling stories about time's cruelty.
"Two weeks," I say. "That's how long they had before the world taught them that bridges were for burning, not building. Before ZIP codes became destiny. Before they learned that in America, you can't always keep what you love, but you can always lose what you never thought to protect."
The demo tape from that last night still plays sometimes in my dreams. Three voices finding harmony so perfect it made the angels weep with envy. Two minutes and forty-seven seconds of proof that we're born knowing how to sing together, and we have to be taught to sing alone.
"Next in our story," I continue, "I'll tell you about the bulletin board that turned harmony into warfare. About how a simple announcement transformed three friends into three armies. About how America took what was beautiful and made it competitive, what was collaborative and made it divisive, what was possible and made it impossible."
But first, one more song from that summer. Not the full demo—that comes later, when the stakes are higher and the irony is complete. Just a fragment, a memory, a ghost of what was before America reminded them what couldn't be.
I slide in the cart labeled "Summer 1974 - Practice Session #3." The tape hisses, the levels are wrong, but through the static you can hear it: three kids who don't know they're supposed to be enemies, making music like it's the most natural thing in the world.
Which it was. Until we taught them it wasn't.








Chapter 4B: The Girl Who Changed Everything
"July 1977," I announce, cueing up Fleetwood Mac's "Don't Stop" because sometimes the obvious choice captures what you need it to capture. "Two best friends, middle school talent show champions, heading to Camp Winaukee with their guitars and their trophy and the absolute certainty that they'd figured out everything important about music, friendship, and life."
They arrived at camp like conquering heroes, at least in their own minds. Nick and Jackie, the Johnson & Marsh duo, carrying the confidence that comes from proving everyone wrong, from showing a room full of people that collaboration could beat competition, that harmony was stronger than tradition.
The camp looked exactly like the brochures had promised: rustic cabins that smelled like pine sap and possibility, a dining hall where food fights were democracy in action, and most importantly for our story, a music building where kids who understood that music mattered more than fitting in could find their tribe.
"The thing about summer camp," I tell my dwindling audience, "is that it's America's laboratory for social experimentation. Take kids away from their familiar hierarchies, their established roles, their predetermined futures, and see what happens when they're free to reinvent themselves for two weeks."
Nick and Jackie were assigned to Cabin 7 again, the music intensive cabin, but this time they arrived as celebrities. Word had gotten around about their talent show victory, about two kids who'd found a way to make Queen and Aerosmith sound like they belonged in the same song, the same friendship, the same world.
The other boys in their cabin treated them with the kind of reverence that thirteen-year-olds reserve for kids who've accomplished something they wish they'd thought of first. Here were two musicians who'd proved that arguing about music was just the warm-up act before making music together.
For the first week, they were inseparable. Best friends exploring their expanded world, discovering that there were kids from all over New England who shared their obsession, their belief that music was salvation disguised as entertainment.
They sat together at meals, practiced together during free periods, explored the hiking trails together while debating whether "Stairway to Heaven" was brilliant or overplayed, whether disco was a legitimate musical form or a sign that America had lost its way.
"But summer camp," I continue, lighting another cigarette, "has a way of introducing variables that even the best friendships aren't prepared for."
Her name was Patty Lally, and she arrived during the second week—a last-minute addition when someone from Vermont had to cancel due to a family emergency. Fourteen years old, wheat-colored hair that caught sunlight like it was storing it for later use, and a voice that made everyone else sound like they were trying too hard.
She wasn't the prettiest girl at camp, but she was the most magnetic. There was something in the way she moved through the world like she belonged wherever she happened to be standing, like confidence was her natural state and self-doubt was a foreign language she'd never bothered to learn.
Patty played guitar like she was having a conversation with it, not trying to impress anyone, just finding the chords that matched whatever mood she happened to be in. Her voice was untrained in the best possible way—no lessons, no technique, no one had ever told her what she was supposed to sound like, so she just sounded like herself.
Both boys noticed. Of course they did. They were thirteen and fourteen, hormones were making all their decisions for them, and here was a girl who could sing like music was something you felt rather than something you performed.
"Here's where the story gets complicated," I tell the darkness beyond the booth. "Because friendship between boys can survive almost anything except competition for the same girl's attention."
It started innocently enough. Nick finding excuses to walk past the girls' cabin during free periods, hoping to catch her practicing. Jackie lingering after meals, positioning himself where he could watch her laugh with her cabin mates, studying her like she was a piece of music he was trying to learn to read.
Neither of them admitted what they were doing, even to themselves. They were best friends. They'd won a talent show together. They were the Johnson & Marsh duo, proof that collaboration was stronger than competition.
But Patty complicated everything just by existing.
During evening practice sessions, she'd sit on the porch of the music building, guitar in her lap, working through songs she'd written about homesickness and summer rain and the particular loneliness of being fourteen in a world designed for people who'd figured out who they were supposed to be.
"Her songs weren't technically impressive," I continue, stubbing out the cigarette with more force than necessary. "They were something better. They were honest. They sounded like what fourteen felt like—too old for childhood, too young for whatever came next, caught in the middle of becoming."
The boys would find reasons to be nearby during her practice sessions. Nick hiding behind trees, Jackie pretending to read on the building's steps, both of them drawn by something they couldn't name but recognized as important.
It was during the third week that everything changed.
Patty had been working on a song about friendship, about how people could be close enough to finish each other's sentences but still mystery to each other in the ways that mattered. She was struggling with the bridge, couldn't find the chord progression that would carry her from the verse to the chorus without losing the melody's emotional thread.
"Need some help?" Nick had asked, emerging from behind his usual pine tree, trying to sound casual while his heart hammered against his ribs like it was trying to escape.
"I could use another guitar," Patty admitted, patting the porch beside her. "Something to fill out the sound."
Jackie appeared from the building's entrance, moving with the careful casualness of someone who'd been waiting for exactly this opportunity. "Mind if I listen?"
So they sat—Nick to her left, Jackie to her right, the girl in the middle like Switzerland mediating between countries that hadn't declared war yet but were considering their options.
Patty started the song again, and this time Nick found a guitar line that supported without overwhelming, blues-based but gentle, like he was trying to prove he could be sensitive when the situation required it.
Jackie listened, then added his voice to hers during the chorus, his classical training helping him find harmonies that made her simple melody suddenly complex, beautiful, complete.
For four minutes, they were perfect together. Three kids making music that none of them could create alone, finding the frequency where different voices could coexist without losing what made them individual.
"That's it," Patty said when they finished, and her smile included both boys equally, democracy in action. "That's exactly what the song needed."
But something had shifted. Nick and Jackie had both heard how good they sounded with her, how their individual talents became something larger when filtered through her honest voice, her unpretentious approach to making music that mattered.
And they'd both seen her smile. The same smile, given to both of them, but somehow each boy convinced himself that his contribution had earned it, that his musical offering had been the one that made the difference.
"The next three days," I tell my audience, reaching for another cigarette, "were a masterclass in how competition can destroy collaboration, how the need to win can make you lose everything that made winning worthwhile."
It wasn't obvious at first. Nick and Jackie were still best friends, still the Johnson & Marsh duo, still proof that different musical philosophies could find common ground. But now they were also rivals for something neither of them was mature enough to understand.
They started volunteering for different camp activities, subtly separating their schedules so they could each have solo time with Patty. Nick would suggest hiking trips, knowing that Jackie preferred indoor activities. Jackie would volunteer for kitchen duty, understanding that Nick considered manual labor beneath someone with his musical ambitions.
During practice sessions, they started trying to outplay each other instead of playing together. Nick would add more complex guitar parts, showing off the blues licks he'd been working on. Jackie would find increasingly sophisticated harmonies, proving his classical training could enhance any musical situation.
Patty noticed, of course. She was fourteen, not blind, and she understood that something was changing between her new friends, something that had more to do with her presence than their music.
"I like both of you better when you're playing together," she said one evening when their competitive dynamic had become too obvious to ignore. "Music isn't supposed to be a contest."
But it was too late. The seed of competition had been planted, and summer camp was almost over. Soon they'd return to their separate worlds, their separate high schools, their separate futures. The question was whether their friendship could survive the transition, or whether the memory of a girl with wheat-colored hair and an honest voice would become the thing that divided them.
"The last night of camp," I continue, "they performed together one final time. Three kids on a makeshift stage, proving that harmony was still possible if everyone agreed to stop competing long enough to listen to each other."
They played Patty's friendship song, the one they'd helped her complete, the one that had inadvertently started their competition. For three minutes and forty-seven seconds, they remembered what they'd been before they started trying to impress someone who was already impressed, before they started competing for attention from someone who was happy to give it freely.
The applause was genuine, enthusiastic, recognition that something real had happened on that small camp stage. But the boys were already thinking about September, about returning to Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School, about how camp friendships translated to the real world where social hierarchies mattered more than musical harmonies.
They exchanged addresses with Patty, promised to stay in touch, swore they'd all get together during the school year. Made the kinds of promises that fourteen-year-olds make when summer is ending and real life is waiting like a patient predator.
"August 1977," I say, stubbing out the cigarette and immediately lighting another. "Two best friends rode home from camp in the back of Grace Johnson's Ford Pinto, arguing about whether Patty had liked Nick's guitar work or Jackie's harmonies better, already forgetting that the question missed the point entirely."
By September, when high school started, they'd convinced themselves that their friendship was still solid, that two weeks of subtle competition hadn't changed anything fundamental between them. They were Nick and Jackie, the Johnson & Marsh duo, middle school talent show champions who'd proven collaboration was stronger than competition.
But high school has a way of reminding kids who they're supposed to be, of sorting them into categories that don't care about summer camp friendships or talent show victories or the brief moment when two boys proved that musical arguments could become musical love songs.
High school would teach them about social hierarchies, about staying in their designated quadrants, about the American talent for turning every collaboration into competition, every friendship into a test of loyalty to ideas they'd never chosen but couldn't escape.
"By Christmas of their freshman year," I tell the darkness, "Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh passed each other in the hallways like strangers who might have shared a dream once but couldn't quite remember the details."
The letters to Patty continued for a few months, but gradually they stopped mentioning each other, stopped talking about their music together, stopped pretending that camp had been anything more than a pleasant interruption in the serious business of becoming who America expected them to be.
But Patty remembered. She kept their addresses, kept their letters, kept the memory of three kids making music that proved harmony was possible between any voices brave enough to sing together instead of alone.
And when she saw that Battle of the Bands poster in March of 1979, she didn't see a competition. She saw two boys who'd forgotten how to harmonize, who needed someone to remind them what they'd once been able to accomplish when they stopped trying to win and started trying to connect.
"Next in our story," I say, reaching for the water glass because my throat is getting rough, "high school happens. Social pressure happens. America happens. Two best friends discover that friendship is a luxury they can't afford, that music is supposed to divide rather than unite, that the frequency they'd found together was just temporary reception in a world designed to generate static."
I slide another cart into the machine, but this time it's different music—something sadder, more minor key, the sound of possibility fading into memory.
"This is what it sounds like when harmony becomes discord. When collaboration becomes competition. When the best friendships become casualties of the American insistence that everything, even music, even love, even the dreams of children, must be a contest with winners and losers."
The music plays, and somewhere in its fading notes is the ghost of two thirteen-year-old boys who'd once proved that difference could be harmony, that arguments could be love songs, that the best music happens when you stop trying to be better than someone else and start trying to be worthy of singing with them.
But that was before. Before high school. Before America reminded them that harmony was a luxury they couldn't afford, that competition was the natural state of the universe, and that the frequency they'd found together was just temporary reception in a world designed to keep people on separate stations.


TIM’S NOTES.  Chapter 6 JAZZ scene before Chapter 5 Bridge Scene … fix chapter numbers later 


Chapter 6: The Teachers Who Fanned the Flames
"Every tragedy needs its supporting cast," I say, watching Boston's skyline shimmer through the booth window like a fever dream built from glass and ambition. "In this one, we had two teachers who couldn't have been more different, yet both managed to make everything worse."
March sixth, 1979. One day after those Battle of the Bands posters appeared throughout Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School, transforming hallway conversations into tribal warfare and turning lunch periods into strategic planning sessions for a conflict nobody fully understood but everyone felt compelled to join.
Bob Wentworth sat in his empty music room after school, staring at the bulletin board where his innocent attempt to save a dying program had unleashed something that felt less like educational initiative and more like social experiment gone wrong. The jazz band was scheduled to practice in twenty minutes, and for the first time in two years, he wasn't sure he wanted them to show up.
"Wentworth was one of those music teachers who'd given up dreams of symphony halls for steady paychecks and summers off," I tell my audience, lighting another cigarette because some characters require combustion to understand properly, "but he'd never quite lost the ability to hear potential in the noise teenagers make. And right now, the noise was getting dangerous."
The lunchroom had reorganized itself into something that looked like democracy but felt like cold war. The Rampers claimed their elevated territory with the territorial certainty of kids who'd never been told they couldn't have something they wanted. The Rats held court in the Glass Corridors, cigarette smoke creating a haze that made everything look like a movie about juvenile delinquency. The Regs scattered throughout the middle ground, trying to avoid choosing sides in a conflict they didn't understand. The Rollers congregated near the parking lot, where they could keep an eye on their motorcycles and muscle cars, mechanical extensions of personalities that valued horsepower over homework.
But the Battle of the Bands posters were scrambling those careful territorial arrangements in ways that made teachers nervous and administrators reach for their antacids. Kids were discovering that their musical preferences didn't align with their geographic assignments, that taste was more complicated than taxonomy, that the frequency they responded to might not be the one their ZIP code was supposed to receive.
Wentworth had watched it happening all day: Rampers arguing about whether technical excellence or emotional authenticity mattered more in rock and roll. Rats debating the relative merits of British sophistication versus American rawness. Regs trying to stake out middle ground between two positions that seemed increasingly incompatible. Rollers discovering that their mechanical aptitude gave them opinions about production values that their social status wasn't supposed to allow.
"The thing about Bob Wentworth," I continue, stubbing out the cigarette in the ashtray that's becoming an archaeological record of this broadcast, "was that he actually believed music could heal divisions. He'd seen it happen in jazz ensembles, watched kids from different backgrounds find common ground in common time signatures. He thought if he could just get Nick and Jackie in the same room, playing the same song, they'd remember what they'd once been to each other."
At 3:47 PM, the jazz band members began filtering into the music room. Seventeen kids who'd chosen musical sophistication over athletic achievement, academic excellence over social prominence, the kind of teenagers who understood that some frequencies were worth listening for even if they made you unpopular.
Nick arrived first, guitar case slung over his shoulder, looking like he'd rather be anywhere else but unable to quit something he'd been part of since sophomore year. Jazz band was where he could play music that required thinking, where his working-class background didn't matter as much as his ability to find the right notes at the right time.
Jackie entered five minutes later, moving with the careful casualness of someone who'd learned to make everything look effortless, even when it required tremendous effort. He'd been studying jazz theory since middle school, understood chord progressions like other kids understood sports statistics, could improvise solos that sounded like they'd been written by someone much older and much more experienced with heartbreak.
For two years, they'd managed to coexist in the jazz ensemble by carefully not existing in the same musical space. They played together but never talked, found harmonies in performance but maintained discord in conversation, proved that collaboration was possible even between people who'd forgotten how to be friends.
But today, with the Battle of the Bands forty-eight hours away and the entire school choosing sides in their dissolution, Wentworth decided to force a reconciliation.
"Gentlemen," he announced to the seventeen-piece band, clutching his conductor's baton like a magic wand that could make this work, "we're going to try something different today. Johnson and Marsh, I want you front and center. We're going to work on a dual arrangement of 'Take Five.'"
The rest of the jazz band exchanged looks—the kind teenagers perfect when adults are about to do something catastrophically naive, when good intentions collide with reality and produce the kind of wreckage that makes everyone uncomfortable.
Nick and Jackie moved to the front of the room like prizefighters being told to touch gloves before trying to destroy each other. Nick carried his guitar like a shield against whatever was about to happen. Jackie approached the piano like it might explode if he played the wrong chord.
"Now," Wentworth continued, his voice carrying the desperate enthusiasm of a teacher who'd run out of conventional options, "I want you to find where Brubeck meets your own... idioms. Johnson, bring the Aerosmith influence. Marsh, bring the Queen sensibility. Show us how jazz can bridge any gap."
In the booth, I laugh, but it's not because it's funny. It's because I recognize the particular blindness that affects adults when they're trying to fix problems they don't understand, when they think technique can solve issues that require emotional surgery.
"'Take Five' is in 5/4 time," I explain to listeners who might not know. "Already about being off-balance, about finding beauty in the unexpected. Asking those two boys to reimagine it through the lens of their musical war was like asking them to translate their pain into a language everyone could dance to, even though dancing wasn't the point anymore."
They started to play. Jackie's classical training made the unusual time signature easy—his left hand finding Brubeck's rhythm while his right hand began adding flourishes that were pure Freddie Mercury, theatrical runs that transformed jazz sophistication into something more operatic, more emotionally expansive.
Nick listened, found the spaces between Jackie's notes, and started threading Aerosmith's blues-based rock through the jazz structure. His guitar work was careful at first, tentative, like someone testing the ice on a pond that might not support his weight.
For thirty seconds, it worked. For thirty seconds, Bob Wentworth was right and music could heal anything. The two boys weren't looking at each other, but they were listening, and in that listening was the ghost of what they'd been at thirteen—two kids who could make magic when they stopped thinking about why they shouldn't.
Then Mr. Don Gould walked in.
Gould taught AP American History, coached the debate team, and considered himself a maverick educator because he wore jeans on Fridays and encouraged his students to call him "Don" during informal discussions. He was thirty-four, had a master's degree from Harvard, and believed that everything was a teachable moment if you were just clever enough to frame it correctly.
"Sounds like democracy in action," he announced, his voice loud enough to break the spell that had been forming between two boys who'd almost remembered how to harmonize. "The American experiment playing out in real time. You know what this reminds me of? The Lincoln-Douglas debates. Same fundamental tension, different century."
Nick's fingers stumbled on the fretboard. Jackie's right hand fell silent, leaving only the bass line carrying a rhythm that suddenly felt lonely.
"Think about it," Gould continued, warming to his theme because Don Gould loved nothing more than the sound of his own pedagogical relevance echoing through rooms full of teenagers who were trying to navigate their own lives without turning everything into a history lesson.
"Johnson here represents the Jacksonian tradition—man of the people, authentic democracy, rough edges and all. Marsh embodies more of the Jeffersonian ideal—educated, refined, believing that excellence elevates everyone around it. Both American traditions! Both valid perspectives on what this country should be!"
"We're just playing music," Nick said, but his voice carried danger now, the particular edge that comes from being reduced to a symbol when you're trying to be a person.
"Are you though?" Gould asked, doing that thing teachers do when they think they're Socrates but sound more like someone who's read too many education journals and not enough actual books. "Or are you playing out the eternal American tension between populism and intellectualism, between—"
"Between the kids who matter and the kids who don't?"
