Nick interrupted, standing up so quickly his guitar strap caught on the music stand, sending sheet music cascading to the floor like snow that had given up trying to be beautiful. "Between the ones whose parents can donate new instruments to the music program and the ones whose parents die in industrial accidents that get blamed on worker error?"
The jazz band had gone completely silent. Seventeen teenagers watching a car crash in slow motion, understanding that they were witnessing something that would be talked about in hallways for weeks, something that would become part of the school's mythology about the year music turned into warfare.
"That's not what I—" Gould started, his face showing the confusion of an adult who'd thought he was facilitating dialogue but had accidentally detonated something much more dangerous.
"You want to make us a lesson?" Nick continued, unplugging his guitar with movements that were precise, controlled, furious. "Fine. Here's your lesson. Jackie's going to Juilliard or Yale or wherever kids like him go when they're done slumming with real music. I'm going to work construction like my dad, maybe get lucky and die quick like him too. That American enough for your educational purposes?"
"Nick," Jackie said quietly, and it was the first time he'd said Nick's name in two years, the first time his voice had carried anything other than careful politeness when addressing the boy who'd once been his best friend.
"What?" Nick turned to face him directly, all the careful distance they'd maintained dissolving into raw confrontation. "You want to pretend this is about music? Like we're equals because we can both play some notes? Your dad made it pretty clear on that bridge yesterday. These boys don't understand boundaries."
"That's not what I—" Jackie started, but Nick was already packing his guitar, already moving toward the door, already proving that some conversations are too late before they even begin.
"Yeah? Then why'd you move? Why'd you step aside like a good little prince letting the peasants pass?"
"Because I was trying to avoid this!" Jackie gestured at the space between them, at the unbridgeable everything that had opened up like a chasm in the music room floor. "Because I thought maybe we could get through one week without—"
"Without what? Without admitting that Hop Brook might as well be the Atlantic Ocean? Without pretending that your Queen covers aren't just you slumming with the real people?"
"Slumming?" Jackie's voice cracked, and suddenly he wasn't the professor's son or the classical prodigy or the kid who was destined for better things. He was just seventeen and confused and tired of being blamed for advantages he'd never asked for but couldn't afford to give up.
"You think this is slumming? You think I want—" He stopped, looked around the room full of their classmates, their teachers, their judges. "You know what? Forget it. You want a war? Fine. Friday night, we'll see whose America wins."
He left then, didn't storm out so much as evacuated with dignity intact, the way his parents had taught him to exit situations that had become beneath his dignity. The piano bench scraped against the floor, a harsh note that hung in the air like an accusation.
Gould looked around the room at the wreckage of his teachable moment, at the seventeen teenagers who were staring at him like he'd just burned down something they'd been trying to build. "I was just trying to provide historical context—"
"Don," Wentworth said, and his voice carried the exhaustion of every teacher who'd ever watched good intentions detonate in a room full of kids who were trying to figure out how to be human beings while adults insisted on turning their lives into educational opportunities.
"Get out."
Back in 2024, I drain another glass of water and think about the particular damage that well-meaning adults can inflict when they try to solve problems they don't understand, when they mistake intellectual frameworks for emotional realities, when they think that naming something is the same as healing it.
"Bob Wentworth tried to build a bridge with music," I tell the darkness beyond the booth. "Don Gould tried to build one with historical analysis. Both failed because you can't bridge a divide by explaining it, can't heal a wound by describing its characteristics, can't make harmony happen by pointing out that it should be possible."
The jazz band never quite recovered from that afternoon. They still met, still practiced, still performed at the spring concert. But something had been broken that couldn't be fixed with better arrangements or more practice time. The magic that happens when individual musicians become a single organism had been replaced by the careful professionalism of people who were doing their jobs without expecting miracles.
"March seventh, 1979," I continue, "the day after the jazz band explosion, Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh walked the hallways of Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School like former friends who'd discovered they were actually enemies, who'd been pretending to coexist when they should have been preparing for war."
The Battle of the Bands was forty-one hours away. The school was choosing sides not just in a musical competition, but in the dissolution of something it had once celebrated, something that had proved harmony was possible between kids from different sides of anything.
But harmony, it turned out, was more fragile than anyone had realized. More vulnerable to adult interference, peer pressure, and the American insistence that everything, even friendship, even music, even the dreams of teenagers, must be reduced to competition with clear winners and obvious losers.
"Coming up," I tell my invisible audience, reaching for another cigarette, "the bridge where friendship officially died. The moment when two former best friends chose territory over connection, division over recognition, the safety of their assigned corners over the risk of remembering what they'd once been able to accomplish together."
The ON AIR sign pulses steadily, a mechanical heartbeat counting down toward midnight, toward silence, toward the end of WBCN and everything it represented about the possibility that music could matter more than the markets that consumed it.
But first, we need to talk about water that divides, bridges that don't connect, and the particular American talent for turning every shared space into contested territory where the only choice is to claim your side or lose everything you never knew you had.


Chapter 5: The Bridge Where Friendship Died
"March seventh, 1979," I announce, cueing up The Who's "Won't Get Fooled Again" because Pete Townshend understood something about how revolutions become the same old oppression with different managers. "Two days after those Battle of the Bands posters appeared throughout Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School, and the entire building was buzzing with the kind of energy usually reserved for snow days or fire drills."
The posters weren't just in the music room anymore. Principal Sargent, recognizing that something significant was happening, had authorized them to be displayed in the Glass Corridors—those enclosed walkways where smoking was still legal and the Rats congregated between classes, leather jackets and concert t-shirts their uniform of rebellion against a world that had already decided they didn't matter.
Additional posters went up near the cafeteria, close enough to the Ramp—that elevated section where the Rappers held court—that everyone had to see them whether they cared about music or not. The Ramp was sacred territory, tables reserved for kids whose parents drove BMWs and took European vacations, who wore Izod shirts and Top-Siders like membership cards in a club nobody else could afford to join.
"Here's what you need to understand about those posters," I tell my audience, lighting another cigarette because some stories require continuous combustion. "They weren't just announcing a competition. They were announcing the end of something that half the school remembered and the other half had heard stories about."
Because Lincoln-Sudbury remembered Nick and Jackie. Not as class representatives or musical archetypes, but as Johnson & Marsh, the middle school duo who'd won the talent show in 1977 with their impossible fusion of Queen and Aerosmith, who'd proved that friendship could be stronger than musical differences, that collaboration could create something more beautiful than competition.
The Rats remembered them practicing in the Glass Corridors during lunch, two kids with acoustic guitars finding harmonies while cigarette smoke created a haze that made everything look like a dream sequence. The Rappers remembered them too, though they'd pretended not to care at the time, remembered two boys who'd briefly made musical sophistication and working-class authenticity sound like they belonged in the same song.
Now those same posters were forcing everyone to choose sides in the dissolution of something they'd all witnessed, all believed in, all secretly hoped might survive the social pressure that high school applied like a vise to anything that didn't fit predetermined categories.
"Tuesday afternoon," I continue, "unseasonably warm for March, the kind of weather that makes teenagers restless and adults nervous. Nick Johnson was riding the mini-bike trails with his usual crew—not the Lincoln boys this time, they had detention—but with Tommy Morrison and Steve Kowalski, two Rats who understood that freedom required small engines and large territories to explore."
The trails wound through the conservation land between the two Sudburys like scars that refused to heal, carved by a decade of kids who'd discovered that mobility didn't depend on parents who worked double shifts. Honda 50s and Yamaha 60s, bikes purchased with lawn-mowing money and birthday cash, ridden by kids who understood that the difference between transportation and escape was often just a matter of horsepower and attitude.
Nick was leading, as usual, his rebuilt Honda CR125 singing that two-stroke song that sounds like controlled violence, like the mechanical equivalent of rock and roll when it stopped worrying about being pretty and started caring about being real. Behind him, Tommy and Steve followed on their own machines, older bikes that leaked oil and burned blue smoke but still carried their riders away from whatever they were trying to avoid.
The main trail crossed Hop Brook via a wooden bridge that was older than any of their families' time in Sudbury, built by the Civilian Conservation Corps during the Depression when the government still believed that work was dignity and dignity was worth preserving. The bridge was ten feet wide, designed for hikers and horseback riders, not motorized vehicles, but motorcycle laws had always been more like suggestions in the conservation land.
Jackie was standing in the middle of that bridge.
Not walking across, not fishing from the side, just standing there like he was waiting for something, looking down at the water that divided his world from Nick's, his future from Nick's past, everything he was supposed to become from everything Nick was supposed to remain.
"You need to move," Nick said, killing the engine but not dismounting, his voice carrying two years of careful distance, of hallway avoidance, of pretending that the person standing ten feet away wasn't someone who'd once known every song on his playlist, every worry that kept him awake at night, every dream that felt too big for the house on Dutton Road.
"It's a walking bridge," Jackie replied, not looking up from the water, his voice carrying that careful control he'd learned from watching his father handle faculty meetings, department politics, the delicate negotiations required when you lived in a world where politeness was a weapon disguised as courtesy. "Always has been."
Behind Nick, Tommy and Steve idled their engines, the mechanical heartbeat of boys who understood that this moment was about more than right of way, more than traffic flow, more than who had permission to use which piece of infrastructure. This was about territory, about belonging, about the American talent for turning every shared space into contested ground.
"Lot of things used to be different," Nick said, his hands tightening on the handlebars until his knuckles showed white against the cracked leather grips. "Doesn't mean they can't change."
Jackie finally looked up, and for a moment—I swear this is true, both of them told me later, separately, years apart—they almost smiled at each other. Almost remembered being thirteen and fearless, sitting in Dan Johnson's garage, making Queen and Aerosmith sound like they'd been designed to harmonize. Almost remembered what it felt like to be friends before the world taught them that friendship was a luxury they couldn't afford.
Then a voice called from the woods on Jackie's side of the brook: "Everything okay here?"
Dr. Richard Marsh emerged from the trail that led back toward Concord Road, dressed for his afternoon constitutional in pressed khakis and a Boston University windbreaker, walking stick in hand like he was exploring the Amazon rather than the conservation land that his property taxes helped maintain. He took in the scene—his son facing down three kids on dirt bikes—and his face settled into the expression of a man who'd found evidence supporting his worst assumptions about the world.
"These boys bothering you, son?" he asked, and in that pronoun was everything: the careful othering that turned neighbors into strangers, classmates into competitors, kids who'd once shared music into representatives of incompatible philosophies.
"No, Dad," Jackie said, but something in his voice had changed. The careful control was still there, but now it was mixed with something else—embarrassment, maybe, or fear that his father was about to say something that would make reconciliation impossible, that would turn this moment of potential recognition into another casualty of adult interference.
"I was just explaining about the walking bridge," Jackie continued, his voice already beginning to sound like his father's, already learning the vocabulary of polite exclusion that his education was designed to provide.
Dr. Marsh looked at Nick with the kind of assessment that reduced people to categories, to problems that could be solved through proper application of social pressure. Here was a seventeen-year-old boy on a motorcycle, wearing clothes that had been purchased at K-Mart rather than L.L.Bean, carrying himself with the particular pride that comes from having earned everything you own. Obviously a threat to something, though what exactly wasn't clear.
"Perhaps you boys could find another trail," Dr. Marsh said, his voice carrying the reasonable tone that adults use when they're being unreasonable, the kind of suggestion that sounds like a request but functions like a command. "One more... appropriate for your vehicles."
Nick's hands clenched tighter on the handlebars. Behind him, someone gunned their engine—not threatening, exactly, but not not threatening either, the mechanical equivalent of clearing your throat when someone's talking too much without saying anything worth hearing.
"It's a public trail," Nick said, his voice steady but his eyes showing the particular rage that comes from being treated like a problem that needs solving rather than a person who deserves basic respect. "We got as much right—"
"Rights," Dr. Marsh interrupted, stepping closer to his son, establishing a protective perimeter that made the territorial nature of this confrontation absolutely clear, "come with responsibilities. Something you might understand when you're older."
In the booth, forty-five years later, I pause to take a long sip of water and think about how adults can sharpen class differences into weapons with such casual efficiency, how they can take teenagers who are trying to navigate their own relationship and turn them into symbols of everything that's wrong with America.
"What happened next," I tell my audience, "set the stage for everything that followed."
Jackie moved. Not dramatically, not with any grand gesture, just stepped aside to let the motorcycles pass. A few feet to the right, off the center of the bridge, making space for the mechanical intrusion on pedestrian territory.
It was a small gesture, barely noticeable, the kind of accommodation that civilized people make for each other all the time. But in the economy of teenage pride, it was everything. An acknowledgment that his father's assessment was correct, that these boys were a problem requiring management, that Nick Johnson and his friends were something to be avoided rather than engaged.
Nick could have just ridden past. Could have taken the victory and moved on, accepted the accommodation and continued down the trail toward whatever destination had brought him to the woods in the first place.
Instead, he revved his engine and rode across that bridge at full throttle.
The old wood groaned under the sudden weight and vibration, the sound echoing across the water like a scream that had been building for two years, ever since two best friends had discovered that America had very specific ideas about which kids were supposed to succeed and which were supposed to fail.
His friends followed, three bikes turning the walking bridge into a statement about whose freedom mattered more, whose right to public space was more legitimate, whose America they were living in.
When the sound faded, the silence felt like aftermath. Dr. Marsh put his hand on Jackie's shoulder—not comforting, exactly, but claiming, marking territory, establishing ownership of the boy who would make better choices if left to his own devices.
"Those boys," he said, and this time the pronoun carried even more weight, more distance, more certainty that the gulf between his family and theirs was natural, necessary, proof that the world was organized correctly. "They don't understand boundaries. That's why they'll never amount to anything."
"Dad," Jackie started, but what could he say? That one of those boys had once been his best friend? That they'd made music together that felt like truth? That the boundary wasn't about bridges or trails but about something neither of them had chosen but both were trapped by?
"Come on," Dr. Marsh said, guiding his son back toward the trail that led home, back toward the big house where Jackie's future was being carefully planned by people who understood how the world worked, who knew which opportunities were worth pursuing and which were distractions from more important things.
They walked back toward the northwest quadrant, leaving the bridge scarred by tire tracks that would last until the next heavy rain, physical evidence of the moment when two former friends chose division over recognition, territory over connection, the safety of staying in their designated corners over the risk of remembering what they'd once been able to accomplish together.
"That was the last time Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh were alone together before the Battle of the Bands," I tell my listeners, stubbing out the cigarette with unnecessary force. "The last chance they had to choose connection over division, harmony over discord, the frequency that had once made them inseparable over the static that would define them as enemies."
Back in Sudbury, the posters continued to multiply. In the Glass Corridors, where the Rats gathered to smoke and skip classes, kids who remembered Johnson & Marsh began arguing about which of their former heroes deserved support. On the Ramp, where the Rappers held court over their elevated territory, conversations about musical authenticity versus technical excellence became proxies for larger arguments about class, taste, and what mattered when everything was temporary anyway.
The school was choosing sides in the dissolution of something it had once celebrated, proving that America's talent for division was stronger than its capacity for memory, that competition was more compelling than collaboration, that the story we tell ourselves about meritocracy requires us to forget every example of what becomes possible when we stop keeping score.
"By Thursday," I continue, lighting another cigarette, "those tire tracks on the Hop Brook bridge had become symbolic. The Rats saw them as evidence that public space belonged to everyone, that working-class kids had the right to make noise, to claim territory, to exist loudly in a world that preferred them quiet. The Rappers saw them as proof of everything they'd been taught about the other side—that they didn't respect boundaries, didn't understand consequences, didn't deserve the opportunities that required a more refined approach to living in the world."
What none of them understood was that those tire tracks were just the physical evidence of something larger: the moment when two boys who'd once proved that harmony was possible decided that division was safer, that representing their respective sides mattered more than remembering what they'd once represented together.
"March ninth, 1979," I announce, checking the time because some stories need to breathe between their acts. "Six days until the Battle of the Bands. Six days for Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School to forget that Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh had ever been friends, to reimagine them as symbols of incompatible American dreams, to transform their relationship from evidence that collaboration was possible into proof that competition was inevitable."
The mini-bike trails still wind through the conservation land, though they're officially illegal now and the bridge has been replaced twice—once due to normal wear, once because someone decided that the old CCC construction was insufficient for modern safety standards.
But the scars remain. In the earth, where grass still refuses to grow along the paths that kids carved with small engines and large dreams. In the community, where the memory of two friends becomes the mythology of eternal enemies. In the story we tell ourselves about what becomes possible when we stop worrying about boundaries and start caring about the music we can make together, if only we can remember how to listen to the same song.
"Coming up," I tell the darkness beyond the booth, "the final rehearsals. The practice sessions that became political rallies. The moment when music stopped being about harmony and started being about proving which side of Hop Brook understood what America was really supposed to sound like."
The ON AIR sign pulses steadily, counting down toward midnight, toward silence, toward the end of WBCN and everything it represented about the democratic possibility of radio, about frequency that could cross any divide if someone was brave enough to keep broadcasting.
But first, we need to talk about how preparation becomes performance, how practice becomes politics, and how two boys who'd once shared everything except their musical preferences discovered they couldn't even share the same stage without turning harmony into warfare.

Chapter 7: When Music Scrambled the Social Order
"March eighth, 1979," I announce, cueing up The Clash's "White Man in Hammersmith Palais" because sometimes you need British punk to explain American class warfare. "The morning after the jazz band explosion, and Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School woke up to discover that music had done something unprecedented: it had scrambled the social order."
For three days, those Battle of the Bands posters had been forcing kids to make a choice that their geography hadn't prepared them for. Not just Nick versus Jackie—that was personal, specific, the dissolution of something everyone remembered. This was bigger: Aerosmith versus Queen. Raw versus refined. Working-class authenticity versus educated sophistication. American grit versus British theatricality.
And the choice was cutting across territorial lines in ways that made guidance counselors reach for their coffee and administrators wonder if they needed to call in social workers to handle whatever was happening in their carefully managed educational ecosystem.
"Here's what you need to understand about high school social hierarchies," I tell my dwindling audience, lighting another cigarette because some observations require combustion to be properly digested. "They work perfectly until someone introduces a variable that doesn't respect the established boundaries. Music—real music, music that matters—is that variable."
Let me paint you the picture of Lincoln-Sudbury's social geography, because it matters to understand what was being disrupted.
The Ramp dominated the cafeteria like a fortress of privilege, elevated six inches above the common eating area by architectural design that might have been accidental but functioned like intentional segregation. The Rampers held court there with the territorial certainty of kids who'd never been told they couldn't have something they wanted. These were the northwest quadrant elite: Harvard legacy admissions, European summer vacations, SAT prep courses that cost more than most families' grocery budgets.
Sarah Morrison sat at the head table, student council president, her father a federal judge, her mother on the hospital board. She'd been listening to classical music since she could hold her head up, had seen the Boston Symphony Orchestra from the family's season subscription seats, understood that cultural sophistication was both birthright and obligation.
But Sarah had been secretly playing Queen's "News of the World" album in her bedroom, volume low enough that the help wouldn't hear, drawn to something in Freddie Mercury's voice that her conservatory training had never taught her to recognize: the sound of someone who refused to be contained by other people's definitions of what music should be.
The Glass Corridors reeked of cigarette smoke and teenage rebellion, enclosed walkways where the Rats congregated between classes and sometimes instead of classes. These were the northeast quadrant kids: fathers who worked construction or assembly lines, mothers who cleaned houses or worked retail, families that measured success in overtime pay and mortgage payments that stayed current.
Tommy Morrison—no relation to the judge's family, despite sharing a name—leaned against the windows that gave the corridors their name, leather jacket worn thin at the elbows, Aerosmith patches covering holes that had been earned through actual wear rather than purchased distressed. He'd been to three Aerosmith concerts, knew every Steven Tyler scream, every Joe Perry guitar lick, understood that rock and roll was supposed to be dangerous, real, uncompromising.
But Tommy had borrowed his girlfriend's copy of "A Night at the Opera" and found himself listening to "Bohemian Rhapsody" on repeat, fascinated by music that was simultaneously operatic and rock, sophisticated and raw, proving that complexity didn't have to mean compromise.
The Regulars scattered throughout the middle territories of cafeteria and classroom, kids from the southwest quadrant who understood that their parents' academic careers provided cultural capital but not necessarily financial security. These were professors' children, teachers' kids, families that owned books but not boats, that valued education but couldn't always afford its application.
Lisa Henderson's father taught English at Framingham State, her mother worked part-time at the library. They had classical records and folk albums, subscribed to The New Yorker and listened to NPR, understood that culture mattered but didn't always agree about what culture should include.
Lisa found herself drawn to both sides of the musical argument, hearing the poetry in Steven Tyler's lyrics and the theatrical ambition in Freddie Mercury's compositions, wondering why appreciation had to be exclusive, why choosing one meant rejecting the other.
The Rollers claimed the parking lot as their natural habitat, gathered around motorcycles and muscle cars that were mechanical extensions of personalities that valued horsepower over homework. These were the southeast quadrant kids: rural backgrounds, agricultural families, mechanical aptitude that colleges didn't know how to measure but employers desperately needed.
Danny Kowalski's father ran the auto repair shop on Route 20, his hands permanently stained with grease and transmission fluid. Danny could rebuild an engine by fifteen, understood that power came from precision, that mechanical excellence required both technical knowledge and intuitive understanding of how things worked.
He'd been listening to both Aerosmith and Queen, not because of their social significance but because both bands understood that rock and roll was about power—different kinds of power, but power nonetheless. Aerosmith's was raw, immediate, the sound of American machinery. Queen's was controlled, theatrical, the sound of precision engineering applied to emotional expression.
"March eighth through tenth," I continue, stubbing out the cigarette and immediately lighting another, "those four social groups discovered that their musical preferences didn't align with their geographic assignments. And for seventy-two hours, Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School experienced something that looked suspiciously like democracy."
The scrambling started in the most unlikely place: AP Chemistry, where Sarah Morrison found herself paired with Tommy Morrison (the seating chart arranged alphabetically, indifferent to social hierarchies). They were working on molecular structures when Tommy noticed the Queen lyrics written in Sarah's notebook margins.
"You like Mercury?" he asked, his voice carrying the surprise of someone discovering that gravity might work differently than expected.
"You know Queen?" Sarah replied, equally surprised to find common ground with someone from the Glass Corridors.
They spent the rest of chemistry class arguing about whether "We Will Rock You" was brilliant minimalism or lazy songwriting, whether Freddie Mercury's operatic training made Queen more sophisticated than Aerosmith or just different. By the end of class, they'd discovered that musical taste was more complicated than ZIP codes, that appreciation could cross territorial boundaries if you were brave enough to admit what you actually heard instead of what you were supposed to hear.
The Regulars found themselves in the middle, literally and figuratively, as Lisa Henderson started sitting with different groups depending on which aspect of the musical argument she wanted to explore. Lunch with the Rampers when she wanted to discuss Queen's compositional complexity. Study hall with the Rats when she wanted to understand Aerosmith's emotional authenticity.
She was conducting her own musical anthropology project, discovering that both sides had valid points, that the argument wasn't really about Queen versus Aerosmith but about competing definitions of what mattered: technical excellence or emotional truth, sophisticated arrangement or raw power, British theatricality or American directness.
The Rollers remained characteristically pragmatic, evaluating both bands on mechanical terms that had nothing to do with social significance. Danny Kowalski organized listening sessions in the automotive shop, playing Queen and Aerosmith albums while his friends worked on their cars, judging the music by how well it fit their mechanical rhythms.
"Listen to this," he'd say, cueing up "Bohemian Rhapsody" while Billy Chen synchronized his engine timing. "Six minutes, no chorus, five different sections. Like building a transmission—every part has to be perfect or the whole thing fails."
Then he'd switch to "Walk This Way," pointing out how the simplicity was deceptive, how three chords could generate more power than six-part harmonies if you understood the engineering. "Sometimes the best design is the one that looks simple but works perfectly."
"But here's where it got interesting," I tell my audience, reaching for my water because my throat is getting rough from chain-smoking through this story. "The adults noticed."
Guidance counselors started seeing unusual combinations in their offices: Rampers and Rats discussing musical theory, Regulars and Rollers finding common ground in automotive acoustics, traditional social boundaries blurring in ways that made professional educators nervous.
Mrs. Patterson—the guidance counselor who specialized in college placement—called several parents to report that their children were "expanding their social circles in potentially problematic ways." Code for: your kid is talking to people from the wrong side of town, and it might affect their college applications if admissions committees think they lack proper social judgment.
Dr. Marsh received one of those calls.
"Jackie has been spending time with some... unusual classmates," Mrs. Patterson reported during a Thursday afternoon phone conference. "Nothing inappropriate, you understand, but perhaps not the kind of associations that demonstrate the leadership qualities that Juilliard expects to see."
The call lasted eight minutes. Dr. Marsh spent most of it listening, asking careful questions, taking notes in the precise handwriting that had served him through graduate school and faculty meetings and the careful navigation of academic politics.
That evening, over dinner in the dining room that overlooked the conservation land where mini-bike trails scarred the landscape like evidence of trespassing, Dr. Marsh addressed his son with the careful precision of someone who'd spent his career managing delicate situations.
"I understand you've been having interesting conversations about music," he said, cutting his salmon with surgical precision. "With students from... various backgrounds."
Jackie understood immediately. The brief musical democracy was over. The experiment in cross-class appreciation had been noticed, evaluated, and found potentially damaging to his carefully planned future.
"It's just music, Dad," Jackie said, but his voice already carried the resignation of someone who understood that "just music" wasn't something his family could afford to risk.
"Music is never just music," Dr. Marsh replied. "It's cultural positioning. It's social signaling. It's preparation for the associations you'll make in college, graduate school, your professional career. The people you're talking to now—they're not going where you're going."
Similar conversations were happening in houses throughout Sudbury. Grace Johnson, exhausted from her double shift at Friendly's, finding energy to lecture Nick about "not getting distracted by rich kids who are just slumming with real people." The Hendersons discussing the importance of "maintaining focus on academic achievement rather than social experimentation." The Kowalskis reminding Danny that "mechanical skills matter more than musical opinions."
"March eleventh, 1979," I continue, "the social gravity reasserted itself. The brief musical democracy collapsed back into familiar territories. Kids who'd been discovering common ground across class lines suddenly remembered why those lines existed, why crossing them was dangerous, why their parents had spent years teaching them which associations were safe and which were potentially damaging to their futures."
By Monday, the cafeteria had returned to its normal configuration. The Rampers reclaimed their elevated territory, the musical experimentation relegated to private listening rather than public discussion. The Rats retreated to the Glass Corridors, where conversations about Queen were limited to acknowledging that "some of their songs weren't terrible, for British music." The Regulars scattered back to their middle territories, the anthropological project abandoned. The Rollers returned to their parking lot, where musical discussions were limited to which songs worked best as soundtracks for automotive projects.
The posters were still there, still advertising the Battle of the Bands. But now they functioned differently—not as invitations to musical discovery, but as battle flags for a war where choosing sides was mandatory and neutrality was impossible.
"What died that week," I tell the darkness beyond the booth, "wasn't just Nick and Jackie's friendship. It was the brief possibility that music could matter more than geography, that appreciation could transcend territory, that American kids could choose their own cultural allegiances instead of inheriting them from their ZIP codes."
The Battle of the Bands was four days away. Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School had tried democracy and found it too dangerous to sustain. The brief scrambling of social boundaries had been corrected, the experimental cross-class appreciation had been discouraged, the musical curiosity had been channeled back into safer, more predictable expressions of tribal loyalty.
"But for seventy-two hours," I say, lighting what might actually be my last cigarette of this section, "music had proved what it could do if we let it. If we didn't teach kids that their taste had to match their territory, that their appreciation had to align with their advantages, that their curiosity had to be contained within the borders their parents had drawn around their possible futures."
For seventy-two hours, Sarah Morrison and Tommy Morrison had shared Queen lyrics in chemistry class. Lisa Henderson had discovered that both sides of the musical argument had merit. Danny Kowalski had applied mechanical precision to artistic appreciation. Kids from four different quadrants had found common ground in uncommon music.
Then America reminded them that common ground was a luxury they couldn't afford, that musical democracy was a dangerous experiment, that the safest choice was to stay in their designated corners and cheer for their assigned teams in a competition that was never really about music at all.
"Coming up," I tell my invisible audience, "the bridge where it all ended. The moment when two former best friends chose territory over recognition, division over connection, the safety of their assigned roles over the risk of remembering what they'd once been able to accomplish when they stopped worrying about which side of Hop Brook they belonged on and started caring about the frequency they could find together."
The ON AIR sign continues its steady pulse, counting down toward midnight, toward silence, toward the end of WBCN and the particular kind of radio democracy that had once made musical discovery possible for kids who couldn't afford to discover it anywhere else.
But first, water that divides. Bridges that don't connect. And the American talent for teaching children that the most dangerous thing they can do is admit they hear beauty in the wrong kind of music, appreciate excellence from the wrong kind of people, find harmony across the wrong kind of boundaries.

Chapter 8: The Day Grace Lost Her Grace
"March tenth, 1979," I announce, stubbing out my cigarette and immediately lighting another because some stories require continuous combustion to tell properly. "Three days before the Battle of the Bands, and I need to tell you about the day Grace Johnson finally said what everyone in Sudbury knew but nobody wanted to acknowledge."
I wasn't there when it happened. I heard about it later, the way you hear about legendary moments that become part of a community's mythology—not because they're pleasant to remember, but because they're too true to forget. The story came to me in pieces: from Chuck Morrison, who'd been having coffee at the counter when it started. From Dr. Patterson, the pediatrician who'd been in booth four with his wife when the explosion happened. From three teenagers who'd been sharing a sundae and found themselves witnesses to something that would be talked about in hallways for weeks.
But mostly, I heard it from Grace herself, three months later, when she came to the station to thank me for giving Nick his chance at recording, even though nothing had come of it, even though the Battle of the Bands had ended the way these things always end in America—with everyone more divided than when they started.
"I never lost my temper at work," she told me that June afternoon, sitting in the same chair where her son had sat, where Jackie Marsh had sat, where dozens of kids had sat hoping I could make their music matter to someone besides themselves. "Twenty-three years serving people their coffee and their complaints, and I never once let them see what I really thought about their casual cruelties."
Friendly's occupied a corner of Route 20 like it had been there since the Pilgrims invented ice cream, though actually it had been built in 1962 as part of the New England chain that promised family dining and wholesome values in an era when both seemed endangered by social change. Red vinyl booths, black and white checkered floor, a counter where you could sit and watch the grill cook while you decided whether pie was worth the calories.
Grace had worked there since 1970, hired three months after Nick was born, back when Dan was still alive and the future looked like something they could plan for instead of just survive. She'd started as weekend help, moved to full-time after Dan died, picked up doubles when the bills demanded it, which was most of the time.
"The thing about serving people," I tell my dwindling audience, pouring another glass of water because my throat is raw from smoke and truth, "is that you become invisible. They talk around you like you're furniture, like you don't have opinions or feelings or children whose futures they're casually discussing over their coffee and pie."
March tenth was a Saturday, lunch rush in full swing, every booth filled with families who'd driven in from all four quadrants of Sudbury to discuss the Battle of the Bands that had transformed their quiet community into something that felt like a sociological experiment gone wrong.
Grace was working a double—had been since six AM, would stay until closing at ten PM because the money was too necessary to turn down and because staying busy meant less time to worry about Nick, about the competition that was consuming him, about the pressure he was carrying to represent every working-class kid who'd ever been told their dreams were too big for their circumstances.
Table six: Dr. and Mrs. Marsh, having lunch with another faculty couple from Boston University. Academic voices, careful enunciation, the kind of conversation that sounded like a seminar even when they were discussing the weather.
"Jackie's been working so hard," Mrs. Marsh was saying, cutting her Cobb salad with surgical precision. "The competition is bringing out the best in him. Nothing like a challenge to reveal character."
"I just hope he's not taking it too seriously," Dr. Marsh replied, his voice carrying the measured concern of someone who'd never had to worry about taking anything too seriously because success had always been expected rather than hoped for. "It's high school music, after all. The important thing is that it prepares him for Juilliard."
Grace refilled their water glasses, invisible and efficient.
"What about the other boy?" asked Dr. Henderson from the English department. "The Johnson child? I hear he's quite... intense about this competition."
"Well," Mrs. Marsh lowered her voice to the register people use when discussing unfortunate circumstances, "you have to understand his background. Single mother, working-class family, father died in that construction accident. Music is probably his only chance for... advancement."
Grace's hand trembled slightly as she poured, but her professional smile never wavered.
Booth twelve: Sarah Morrison and three other Rampers, home from their Saturday morning SAT prep course, discussing the social dynamics of supporting different sides in the musical competition.
"I mean, I feel bad for Nick," Sarah was saying, adding Sweet'N Low to her Diet Coke with the careful precision of someone who'd been calorie-counting since middle school. "He's obviously talented, but talent isn't everything. Jackie has training, you know? Professional instruction. It's not really fair to compare them."
"That's what makes it interesting," replied Jennifer Walsh, whose father owned the BMW dealership and whose opinion about fairness had been shaped by never having to experience its absence. "It's like watching natural ability compete against proper education. Very American."
Grace wiped down nearby tables, her movements mechanical, professional, increasingly furious.
The counter: Chuck Morrison from WBCN, having coffee with Bob Walker from Strawberries Records, discussing the competition from their perspective as judges and industry professionals.
"The Marsh boy has real potential," Walker was saying, stirring sugar into his coffee with the confidence of someone whose opinion mattered in circles that determined careers. "Classical training, excellent equipment, supportive family. The kind of foundation you need for a serious musical career."
"What about the Johnson kid?" Chuck asked, and Grace stopped pretending to clean the already-clean counter, stopped moving entirely.
"Raw talent, no question. But talent without resources..." Walker shrugged, the gesture containing everything unsaid about how the music industry worked, who got opportunities and who got overlooked. "It's tragic, really. In a different world, with different circumstances, he might have had a shot."
Grace set down her cleaning cloth, her movements careful, controlled, the way you move when you're trying not to break something you can't afford to replace.
Booth four: Dr. Patterson, the pediatrician, with his wife and teenage daughter, discussing the competition as an example of healthy community involvement, their conversation carrying the benevolent condescension of people who believed their good intentions absolved them of examining their assumptions.
"It's wonderful that the school is giving these children a chance to express themselves," Mrs. Patterson was saying, her voice warm with the kind of charity that costs nothing to give. "Especially for the less fortunate ones. Music can be such a positive outlet for children who don't have other opportunities."
"I just hope it doesn't get too competitive," Dr. Patterson added, cutting his apple pie with the precision that had served him through medical school and private practice. "Some of these families take these things so seriously. As if a high school talent show could actually change their circumstances."
That's when Grace's professional composure finally cracked.
Not dramatically. Not with thrown dishes or raised voices or the kind of scene that would make people uncomfortable. She simply walked over to booth four, coffeepot in hand, and began refilling cups that were already full.
"Excuse me," she said, her voice carrying the careful politeness that had served her through twenty-three years of customer service, but underneath it was something else now, something that made everyone in earshot stop their own conversations and pay attention.
"I'm sorry to interrupt, but I couldn't help overhearing your discussion about the music competition. About how it's such a wonderful opportunity for 'less fortunate' children to express themselves."
The Pattersons looked up, confused by the breach in the invisible barrier between servers and served, unsure how to respond to a waitress who was speaking to them as if she were an actual person with actual opinions.
"I wanted to thank you," Grace continued, her voice getting stronger, more dangerous, like a storm system that had been building pressure for decades and finally found an outlet. "For your concern about children who 'don't have other opportunities.' For your charity toward families who 'take these things so seriously.' For your wisdom about what can and cannot change people's circumstances."
The restaurant was getting quiet now, the kind of silence that happens when people sense something significant is about to occur, when the normal social order is about to be disrupted in ways that will be remembered long after the immediate discomfort fades.
"You see," Grace said, setting the coffeepot down on their table with enough force to make the cups rattle, "I'm one of those mothers who takes this 'too seriously.' My son Nick is one of those 'less fortunate' children who doesn't have 'other opportunities.' And I serve your coffee and your meals and your casual conversations about my family's life like it's entertainment for your Saturday lunch."
Dr. Patterson opened his mouth to say something—probably an apology, possibly an explanation—but Grace wasn't finished.
"For twenty-three years, I've listened to you people discuss my neighbors, my friends, my son, like we're characters in a story you're telling yourselves about how generous and understanding you are. You tip fifteen percent on your check and think you're participating in social justice. You send your children to the same school as mine and think that makes us equal."
Her voice was rising now, not to shouting but to the level where everyone in Friendly's could hear clearly, where the kitchen staff had stopped cooking and the other waitresses had stopped serving and the customers had stopped pretending they weren't listening to every word.
"You want to know what changes circumstances, Dr. Patterson? Working doubles since my husband died so my son can have guitar strings. Choosing between heating oil and music lessons. Watching him practice in a garage that still smells like his father's work clothes because that's the only space we can afford for his dreams."
She picked up the coffeepot again, held it like a weapon that had been disguised as a service tool.
"You think this competition is about music? It's about proving that working-class kids deserve to be heard, not just tolerated. It's about showing people like you that talent doesn't come with a pedigree, that excellence doesn't require your permission or your patience or your charitable concern."
The silence was complete now, the kind that happens when truth has been spoken in a place where it wasn't expected, wasn't welcome, but couldn't be ignored.
"My son has more musical talent in his calloused fingertips than most of your children will ever have in their properly manicured hands. But he'll spend his life proving he deserves what your kids inherit. He'll fight for opportunities your families consider birthright. And people like you will watch from your comfortable booths and discuss whether it's 'healthy' for him to want more than what geography assigned him."
Grace straightened her uniform—the polyester that made everyone look like they were melting under fluorescent lights, the name tag that reduced her identity to first name and corporate cheerfulness, the apron that had absorbed twenty-three years of other people's messes.
"Enjoy your pie, Dr. Patterson. It's made from apples grown in this town, served by people who live in this town, paid for by families who've spent generations making your comfort possible. Maybe think about that the next time you're discussing what's 'realistic' for other people's children to hope for."
She walked away then, not to the kitchen or the counter or anywhere that would suggest she was running from what she'd said. She walked to the next table—booth seven, where a young couple was sharing dessert—and asked if they needed anything else, her voice back to professional courtesy as if nothing had happened, as if she hadn't just demolished the polite fiction that had allowed Friendly's to function as neutral territory in a town divided by every kind of boundary except honesty.
"The manager came out of the office eventually," I tell my audience, lighting another cigarette because Grace's story deserves proper ceremony. "Tom Andrews, fifteen years managing Friendly's, never seen anything like it. He took Grace into the back room, had the conversation that managers have with employees who've violated the cardinal rule of customer service: never make the customers uncomfortable with your humanity."
But Tom Andrews had grown up in Sudbury too. Had worked construction with Dan Johnson, had been to the funeral, had watched Grace carry herself with dignity through circumstances that would have broken most people. He gave her a lecture about professionalism and public relations and the importance of maintaining appropriate boundaries between staff and customers.
Then he gave her a raise.
"Fifteen cents an hour," Grace told me that June afternoon, laughing the way people laugh when the irony is too perfect to be tragic. "Fifteen cents for finally saying what everyone in this town knows but pretends they don't. For proving that the help has opinions, feelings, children whose futures matter as much as anyone else's."
The story spread through Sudbury like spilled coffee, staining everything it touched. By Monday, everyone had heard some version of what had happened at Friendly's, though the details varied depending on who was telling it and what they needed it to mean.
The Rampers' version emphasized Grace's lack of professionalism, her inappropriate boundary-crossing, the discomfort of innocent diners who were just trying to enjoy their Saturday lunch.
The Rats' version celebrated her courage, her refusal to stay invisible, her defense of working-class dignity against academic condescension.
The Regs tried to find middle ground, acknowledging that both sides had valid points while missing the larger truth that some moments require choosing sides rather than finding balance.
The Rollers focused on the practical aspects: Grace kept her job, got a raise, proved that standing up for yourself sometimes worked if you'd earned enough respect to survive the consequences.
But here's what everyone agreed on: something had shifted in Sudbury that Saturday afternoon. The polite fiction that allowed different classes to coexist in the same community had been exposed as fiction. The comfortable assumption that service workers were grateful for their customers' benevolent tolerance had been shattered. The belief that economic inequality could be maintained through charitable concern rather than structural change had been challenged by someone who lived the reality rather than studying it.
"March thirteenth, 1979," I continue, checking the time because some stories have natural stopping points. "Three days after Grace's explosion at Friendly's, and Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School was buzzing with energy that had nothing to do with teenage drama and everything to do with adult realizations about the community they'd created."
The Battle of the Bands was forty-eight hours away. But now it carried weight that transcended high school competition. It had become a referendum on Grace Johnson's speech, a test of whether her challenge to Sudbury's comfortable assumptions would be validated or dismissed, a chance for the community to prove they'd learned something from being forced to confront their own contradictions.
"That's the thing about truth," I tell the darkness beyond the booth, stubbing out the cigarette with unnecessary force. "Once it's been spoken in public, you can't pretend you didn't hear it. You can disagree with it, you can dismiss it, you can try to minimize its importance. But you can't unknow what you know. You can't unfeel what you felt when someone forced you to see yourself through their eyes instead of your own."
Grace Johnson had done more than defend her son. She'd held up a mirror to Sudbury and made everyone look at what they'd been pretending not to see. The question now was whether that recognition would change anything, or whether America's talent for forgetting uncomfortable truths would reassert itself once the immediate discomfort faded.
"Coming up," I tell my invisible audience, "the final rehearsals. The last forty-eight hours before two former best friends took the stage to represent everything America couldn't reconcile about itself. The moment when music stopped being about harmony and started being about proving which side of Hop Brook understood what dignity looked like when it refused to stay quiet."
The ON AIR sign pulses steadily, counting down toward midnight, toward silence, toward the end of WBCN and everything it represented about the possibility that voices could matter more than the markets that determined whose voices got heard.
But first, we need to talk about how preparation becomes performance, how practice becomes politics, and how a single mother's moment of truth turned a high school talent show into something that would define a community's understanding of itself for generations to come.


Chapter 9: The Weight of Representation
"March twelfth, 1979," I announce, cueing up Bob Dylan's "The Times They Are A-Changin'" because sometimes you need the prophet of American folk to explain what happens when private pain becomes public statement. "Forty-eight hours before the Battle of the Bands, and both boys discovered that music had stopped being about music."
Two days after Grace Johnson's controlled explosion at Friendly's, and Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School felt like a community holding its breath, waiting to see whether Saturday night would validate her challenge to their comfortable assumptions or dismiss it as the emotional outburst of a working mother who'd forgotten her place in the social order.
"The thing about being chosen to represent something larger than yourself," I tell my audience, lighting another cigarette because some stories require continuous combustion, "is that you lose the right to fail on your own terms. Every mistake becomes evidence of your people's inadequacy. Every success becomes proof that your side was right all along. And every seventeen-year-old boy carries that weight differently."
Nick hadn't slept since Saturday. Not really slept—he'd closed his eyes, lay in his narrow bed listening to the house settle around him, but actual rest required a kind of peace that had been impossible since he'd heard about his mother's speech from Tommy Morrison, who'd heard it from his older sister, who'd been working the Saturday dinner shift at Friendly's when the story was still spreading through the restaurant like spilled syrup, sticky and impossible to clean up completely.
Monday morning, walking through the Glass Corridors where the Rats gathered between classes, Nick had been treated like something between a celebrity and a martyr. Kids who'd never spoken to him directly were suddenly asking about the Battle of the Bands, about whether he was ready, about whether he understood what was riding on Friday night's performance.
"Your mom's got balls, Johnson," Steve Kowalski had said, his voice carrying the particular respect that working-class kids reserve for adults who'd risked something real for something important. "Taking on those college assholes like that. Making them see what they've been pretending not to notice."
But respect felt heavier than indifference. At least when people ignored you, you could fail without disappointing anyone except yourself. Now Nick was carrying the hopes of every kid whose parents worked doubles, every family that counted pennies between paychecks, every teenage dream that had been dismissed as unrealistic by people who'd never had to choose between realistic and possible.
The garage on Dutton Road had become a pilgrimage site. Not literally—Grace wouldn't allow that, had made it clear that reporters and curiosity seekers weren't welcome—but kids from the northeast neighborhoods kept finding reasons to walk past, to listen to the music bleeding through the walls, to witness the sound of their own hopes being rehearsed in three-chord progressions and four-four time.
"Tuesday evening," I continue, stubbing out the cigarette and immediately lighting another, "Nick stood in that garage surrounded by his father's tools and his mother's expectations and his community's faith, trying to remember why he'd started playing music in the first place."
The Lincoln boys were there—Stephen, David, and Mark, the inseparable trio who'd found in Nick's friendship the fourth corner of a musical square they hadn't known they were building. They'd been practicing for six hours, running through their set list until their fingers bled and their voices cracked, transforming Aerosmith covers into something rawer, more urgent, more honest than the originals.
But something was wrong. The music that had felt like salvation was starting to sound like obligation. The garage that had been sanctuary was feeling like a pressure cooker. The songs that had once been about escape were becoming about proving something to people who'd already decided what they believed about kids from places like this.
"What if we're not good enough?" Mark asked during a break, his usually steady hands shaking as he adjusted the settings on his borrowed keyboard. "What if all this talk about representing working-class kids just makes it worse when we lose?"
"We're not going to lose," Nick said, but his voice carried the hollow certainty of someone trying to convince himself more than his bandmates.
"But what if we do?" Stephen pressed, his bass guitar leaning against his shoulder like a weapon he was too tired to carry. "What happens to your mom then? To all the people who believed in us?"
Nick didn't have an answer for that. Couldn't admit that he'd been asking himself the same question every night since Saturday, lying awake calculating the cost of failure when failure meant more than just losing a high school competition.
Meanwhile, across town on Concord Road, Jackie Marsh was experiencing a different kind of pressure—the weight of representing advantages he'd never asked for but couldn't escape, of proving that privilege and talent weren't mutually exclusive, that his musical education enhanced rather than replaced authentic feeling.
The Marsh basement had been transformed into something resembling a professional recording studio: proper acoustics, high-end equipment, microphones that cost more than most families' monthly grocery budgets. Crown Jewels rehearsed there with the precision of a military operation, every note planned, every harmony arranged, every moment choreographed to demonstrate that technical excellence and emotional truth could coexist in the same performance.
But Jackie had heard about Grace Johnson's speech too—everyone in Sudbury had heard about it—and the knowledge sat in his stomach like a stone, weighing down every song, making every perfectly executed passage sound like evidence of the very inequality his mother's outburst had challenged.
"Wednesday after school," I tell my dwindling audience, "Jackie found himself alone in that basement, playing 'Bohemian Rhapsody' on the Steinway grand, trying to find in Mercury's operatic complexity some answer to questions he didn't know how to ask."
Dr. Marsh had been increasingly present during rehearsals, offering suggestions that felt more like instructions, guidance that sounded more like management. The Battle of the Bands had become his son's audition for a future that had already been planned, a demonstration that proper preparation and family investment produced superior results.
"The key is maintaining artistic integrity while showcasing technical superiority," Dr. Marsh had said during Tuesday's practice session, his voice carrying the measured authority of someone who'd spent his career analyzing other people's work rather than creating his own. "The competition isn't really with other bands—it's with the assumption that authentic music requires authentic struggle. We're proving that excellence can be cultivated, that talent improves with proper support."
But Jackie was seventeen, not a thesis statement. And sitting alone in that perfect basement studio, playing songs that had once felt like rebellion, he couldn't shake the feeling that he was preparing to win an argument that missed the point entirely, to prove something that didn't need proving to people who wouldn't be convinced anyway.
The phone rang at 8:47 PM. Jackie almost didn't answer—he was working on the bridge section of "We Are the Champions," trying to find a way to make Mercury's anthem sound earned rather than assumed—but something about the persistence of the ringing made him pick up.
"Jackie?" The voice was tentative, familiar, impossible.
"Nick?"
Silence stretched between them like the years since they'd been friends, like the geographic distance that was measured in more than miles, like everything that had made this phone call inadvisable, dangerous, potentially devastating to both their carefully constructed positions in the war that was consuming their community.
"I heard about what your mom said at Friendly's," Jackie finally continued, his voice careful, controlled, carrying none of the classical projection he'd been trained to use when speaking mattered. "About the things people were saying about you. About us. About this whole competition."
More silence. Nick could hear classical music in the background—not Queen, something older, more formal, the sound of a house where culture was furniture rather than feeling.
"It's gotten weird, hasn't it?" Nick said finally. "This whole thing. Started as a music competition, turned into... I don't even know what to call it."
"A symbol," Jackie answered immediately, like he'd been thinking about this for weeks, which he had. "We've become symbols of things we never chose to represent. You're authentic struggle, I'm privileged excellence. Two different versions of the American dream, battling it out in a high school cafeteria."
"Is that what we are?" Nick asked, and his voice carried something Jackie hadn't heard since they were thirteen: genuine curiosity about what his former best friend actually thought, rather than what he was supposed to think.
"I don't know what we are anymore," Jackie admitted. "I know what everyone else thinks we are. I know what my dad wants me to be, what your mom needs you to prove, what the whole town is expecting from Friday night. But I don't know what we actually are to each other."
The admission hung between them like a bridge neither was sure they should cross.
"Do you remember," Nick said slowly, "when we wrote that song about the railroad crossing? About trains having to stop and wait for each other to pass?"
"Diamond Crossing," Jackie said immediately. "Yeah, I remember."
"Feel like that's what we're doing now. Sitting at the same intersection, but we can't both move forward. One of us has to win, which means one of us has to lose, and either way the trains don't actually connect. They just... wait for permission to continue in different directions."
Jackie was quiet for a long moment, long enough that Nick wondered if he'd hung up, if the metaphor had been too much, if admitting uncertainty was a luxury neither of them could afford with forty-eight hours left before they had to perform their assigned roles in other people's expectations.
"What if we're both wrong?" Jackie said finally. "What if this whole competition is just... another way to make sure we stay in our designated corners? What if winning means proving we can beat each other instead of proving we can make music that matters?"
"Then we're fucked either way," Nick said, and for the first time in months, Jackie laughed—not the careful, polite sound he'd learned to make at dinner parties and faculty gatherings, but the genuine amusement of someone who recognized truth when he heard it, even when the truth was uncomfortable.
"Yeah," Jackie agreed. "We're fucked either way."
They talked for seventeen more minutes. Not about strategy or set lists or the technical aspects of performing under pressure, but about the weight of carrying other people's dreams, about the impossible mathematics of representing entire communities through three-minute songs, about the loneliness of being chosen to prove things they weren't sure they believed themselves.
When they finally hung up, both boys sat in their respective practice spaces—garage and basement, concrete and carpet, necessity and privilege—and understood that Friday night would determine more than which band won a high school competition. It would decide whether two former best friends could find a way back to each other, or whether America's talent for division was stronger than their shared history of harmony.
"Thursday, March fourteenth," I tell my audience, checking the time because some stories need to breathe between their acts. "Twenty-four hours before the Battle of the Bands, and both boys spent their final day of preparation discovering that the hardest part of competition isn't playing better than your opponent. It's playing well enough to justify the faith that other people have invested in your success."
Nick practiced until his fingers bled, until his voice cracked, until the Lincoln boys finally went home to their own nervous preparations, leaving him alone with his father's tools and his mother's courage and the impossible responsibility of proving that working-class kids deserved more than sympathy.
Jackie played until the neighbors complained about the noise, until his parents finally suggested he get some rest, until the perfect basement studio felt like a beautiful prison where every song sounded like evidence in a trial he'd never asked to attend.
"Twenty-four hours," I repeat, stubbing out another cigarette and reaching for water because my throat is raw from smoke and story. "Twenty-four hours for a community to finish choosing sides, for two teenagers to prepare for a competition that had stopped being about music and started being about which version of America deserved to survive."
The ON AIR sign continues its steady pulse, counting down toward midnight, toward silence, toward the end of WBCN and everything it had once represented about the democratic possibility of radio, about voices that mattered more than the demographics that determined whose voices got heard.
"Coming up," I tell the darkness beyond the booth, "the Battle of the Bands itself. The night when Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School became a battleground for every assumption Americans make about class, talent, and what becomes possible when we stop pretending that geography doesn't determine destiny."
But first, one more song. Not from the battle itself—that comes later, when the stakes are highest and the irony is complete. This is from Thursday night, March fourteenth, 1979. A recording that nobody was supposed to make, that I discovered years later in WBCN's archives, labeled simply "Phone Call - Not for Broadcast."
Because sometimes the most important conversations happen when nobody's supposed to be listening, when two former friends finally admit they're more afraid of winning than losing, more terrified of representing their communities than disappointing them, more worried about justifying other people's faith than surviving their own doubt.
The tape is scratchy, incomplete, but through the static you can hear it: two seventeen-year-old boys trying to remember who they were before America taught them who they were supposed to be, before competition became more important than collaboration, before the frequency that had once connected them became another casualty of the static we create to keep ourselves properly divided.

Chapter 10: The Battle Begins
"March fifteenth, 1979," I announce, cueing up The Who's "Baba O'Riley" because sometimes you need the sound of teenage wasteland to capture the moment when everything changes. "Seven PM at Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School, and the cafeteria had been transformed into something that looked like democracy and felt like war."
The lunch tables were gone, replaced by folding chairs arranged in careful rows that would be ignored the moment the music started. The serving line had been converted into a makeshift backstage area, hidden behind sheets that the drama department had donated to the cause. The stage was just risers from the choir room, but under the rented lights it looked like Madison Square Garden to kids who'd never been farther than Worcester.
Eight hundred people crammed into a space designed for six hundred, and the fire marshal had already made two visits, the second time staying to watch because even municipal officials understood that something significant was happening in the suburban heart of Massachusetts.
"I arrived at six-thirty," I tell my audience, lighting another cigarette because witness requires ceremony. "Chuck Morrison was already there, testing microphones with his signature 'Testing, one-two, one-two, is this thing on?' that had become a Boston catchphrase. Carter Alan was setting up his judging materials with the precision of someone who'd learned to take teenage music seriously. Bob Walker from Strawberries Records sat at the judges' table looking like a man who understood that careers could be made or broken in high school cafeterias if you knew how to listen."
The crowd had sorted itself according to the invisible boundaries that had defined the school since September: Rampers claiming the seats closest to the stage, their elevated status translating to elevated seating. Rats gathering near the exits, ready to escape if things got too civilized for their taste. Regs scattered throughout the middle territories, trying to remain neutral in a conflict that had made neutrality impossible. Rollers clustering near the back, where they could keep an eye on their vehicles in the parking lot and make quick exits if the music got too soft for their mechanical sensibilities.
But something was different tonight. The careful segregation was blurring at the edges, kids sitting with friends who happened to like the same music regardless of which quadrant they called home. The brief musical democracy of the previous week had been officially discouraged, but democracy has a way of reasserting itself when something important is at stake.
Grace Johnson sat in the third row, still wearing her Friendly's uniform because she'd come straight from work, her defiance of Saturday afternoon transformed into quiet dignity that made everyone around her sit a little straighter. Dr. and Mrs. Marsh occupied seats that had been reserved for faculty families, their presence carrying the weight of institutional approval and parental expectation.
And scattered throughout the crowd, I spotted faces that told the real story: kids who'd driven over from Concord and Wayland and Framingham, word having spread that this wasn't just another high school talent show but something larger, more significant, worth witnessing even if you didn't have a dog in the fight.
"Seven-fifteen," I continue, stubbing out the cigarette and immediately lighting another. "Chuck Morrison took the stage to announce the rules, the prizes, and the order of performance. But his opening words carried weight that nobody had expected."
"Ladies and gentlemen," Chuck said, his voice carrying the boundless energy of someone who'd done enough cocaine to power a small city but still believed in the democratic possibility of rock and roll, "welcome to what might be the most important Battle of the Bands in New England history."
The crowd stirred, recognizing that they were being invited to witness something significant rather than endure something obligatory.
"Before we begin," Chuck continued, "I want to acknowledge something that's been building in this community over the past week. This competition started as a way to celebrate musical talent. But music, real music, has always been about more than entertainment. It's been about giving voice to experiences that don't get heard, about proving that excellence can emerge from any neighborhood, any background, any set of circumstances."
He paused, and in that pause was everything that had led to this moment: Grace Johnson's controlled explosion at Friendly's, the brief scrambling of social boundaries, the recognition that this small-town competition had become a referendum on larger American questions about class, opportunity, and what becomes possible when we stop pretending that geography doesn't determine destiny.
"Tonight, eight bands will take this stage. Some of you will hear technical excellence. Others will hear authentic emotion. All of you will hear kids who understand that music matters more than the markets that try to contain it. May the best band win—and may we all remember that 'best' means different things depending on what you're listening for."
7:22 PM - The Jesters
Four freshmen from Lincoln took the stage like they were heading to their own execution, instruments held like shields against whatever was about to happen. Tommy Morrison—no relation to the judge's family—carried his bass with the particular care of someone who'd borrowed it from his cousin and couldn't afford to break it. Billy Chen sat behind a drum kit that had been assembled from three different garage sales, none of the pieces matching but all of them thunderous when played with conviction. Mickey Kowalski approached the microphone like it might bite him.
They opened with The Cars' "My Best Friend's Girl," and for three minutes and forty seconds, eight hundred teenagers remembered why they'd fallen in love with music in the first place. Not because it was sophisticated or meaningful or culturally significant, but because it was fun, because it made you move, because four kids who barely needed to shave could make eight hundred people forget their differences long enough to sing along with a chorus everyone knew.
The applause was genuine, enthusiastic, the kind that said 'you exceeded our expectations and reminded us why we're here.' The Jesters cleared their equipment with the efficiency born of practicing the breakdown as much as the performance, and the real competition could begin.
7:31 PM - Mechanical Breakdown
Detroit Rock City from Wayland took the stage in full KISS regalia—face paint applied with mathematical precision, platform boots that added six inches and subtracted mobility, the complete theatrical costume that proved rock and roll was as much about image as sound.
They launched into "Rock and Roll All Nite" with enthusiasm that made up for what they lacked in precision. The lead singer attempted Gene Simmons' trademark tongue-wagging while delivering vocals that were more theatrical than musical. The guitarist wore Ace Frehley's spaceman silver, though it was already starting to run under the hot stage lights.
Two minutes into their set, catastrophe struck in the form of amplifier failure.
The lead guitar cut out mid-solo, leaving the rhythm section and vocals carrying a song that suddenly sounded naked, incomplete. The guitarist frantically checked cables while maintaining his rock star pose, trying to troubleshoot technical problems while wearing makeup that was becoming more abstract by the minute.
"Keep playing!" Chuck Morrison shouted from the judges' table, understanding that equipment failure was part of rock and roll's authentic experience, that sometimes the show going on was more important than the show going right.
What happened next transformed disaster into legend. The crowd, recognizing that these kids needed help, started slow-clapping in rhythm, eight hundred people providing the beat while Detroit Rock City figured out their technical difficulties. Not mocking applause, not ironic appreciation, but genuine support that turned equipment failure into community collaboration.
